\section{The dyadic $q$-Euler determinant}

\subsection{Definition and $q$-Pochhammer tower}

Fix $0<Q<1$.  Since $Q^D$ is trace class, define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E-def}
 \Ecal(u;Q)=\det(I-uQ^D).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Dyadic $q$-Euler product]\label{p7:thm:E-product}
For every $u\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \Ecal(u;Q)
 &=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-uQ^m)^{a_m}\label{p7:eq:E-mult}\\
 &=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}(uQ^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty.\label{p7:eq:E-tower}
\end{align}
It satisfies the exact renormalization equation
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E-scale}
 \boxed{\ \Ecal(u;Q)=(uQ;Q)_\infty\Ecal(u;Q^2).\ }
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first product is the eigenvalue formula for a Fredholm determinant.  The decomposition $D\simeq N\oplus2D$ gives
\[
 \det(I-uQ^D)=\det(I-uQ^N)\det(I-uQ^{2D})
 =(uQ;Q)_\infty\Ecal(u;Q^2).
\]
Iteration gives \cref{p7:eq:E-tower}; convergence follows from $\sum_{j,n}|u|Q^{2^jn}<\infty$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Lambert series and plethystic logarithm}

For $|u|Q<1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E-log}
 -\log\Ecal(u;Q)
 =\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{u^\ell}{\ell}
 \sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{2^j\ell}}{1-Q^{2^j\ell}}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the ordinary generating function of the zero multiplicities is the dyadic Lambert series
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:a-ogf}
 \boxed{\ \sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_mx^m
 =\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{2^j}}{1-x^{2^j}},\qquad |x|<1.\ }
\end{equation}
The same self-similarity now appears in three transforms:
\[
 a_m\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}}
 \quad\longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sum_{j\ge0}\frac{x^{2^j}}{1-x^{2^j}}.
\]

\section{Finite multiscale Gaussian-binomial formulae}

\subsection{A finite determinant}

For $M\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:EM}
 \Ecal_M(u;Q)=\prod_{m=1}^{M}(1-uQ^m)^{a_m}.
\end{equation}
Put
\[
 J=\Floor{\log_2M},
 \qquad
 L_j=\Floor{\frac{M}{2^j}}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Finite multiscale factorization]\label{p7:thm:finite-E}
One has
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:finite-pch}
 \boxed{\ \Ecal_M(u;Q)
 =\prod_{j=0}^{J}(uQ^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_{L_j}.\ }
\end{equation}
Its degree in $u$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:EM-degree}
 \deg_u\Ecal_M=A(M)=2M-\BinaryDigitSum(M).
\end{equation}
Writing
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:EM-coeff-def}
 \Ecal_M(u;Q)=\sum_{k=0}^{A(M)}(-1)^kC_{M,k}(Q)u^k,
\end{equation}
one has the explicit Gaussian-binomial convolution
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:CMk}
 \boxed{
 C_{M,k}(Q)=
 \sum_{\substack{k_0+\cdots+k_J=k\\0\le k_j\le L_j}}
 Q^{\sum_{j=0}^{J}2^j\binom{k_j+1}{2}}
 \prod_{j=0}^{J}\GaussianBinomial{L_j}{k_j}{Q^{2^j}}.\ }
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Expand $a_m$ as the number of representations $m=2^jn$ and regroup:
\[
 \Ecal_M(u;Q)
 =\prod_{j=0}^{J}\prod_{n=1}^{L_j}(1-uQ^{2^jn}),
\]
which is \cref{p7:eq:finite-pch}.  Its degree is $\sum_jL_j=A(M)$.  Applying \cref{p7:eq:q-binomial} to each factor gives
\[
 (uQ^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_{L_j}
 =\sum_{k_j=0}^{L_j}(-1)^{k_j}
 Q^{2^j\binom{k_j+1}{2}}
 \GaussianBinomial{L_j}{k_j}{Q^{2^j}}u^{k_j}.
\]
Multiplication and collection of the total degree proves \cref{p7:eq:CMk}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Dyadic endpoints and a multiscale factorial}

For $M=2^n-1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:dyadic-L}
 L_j=2^{n-j}-1,
 \qquad
 A(2^n-1)=2^{n+1}-n-2.
\end{equation}
The top $Q$-degree of \cref{p7:eq:EM} is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:B-M}
 B(M):=\sum_{m=1}^{M}ma_m
 =\frac12\sum_{j=0}^{J}2^jL_j(L_j+1).
\end{equation}
At a dyadic endpoint,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:B-dyadic}
 B(2^n-1)=4^n-(n+2)2^{n-1}.
\end{equation}
The classical limit of the normalizing product is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:multiscale-factorial}
 \lim_{Q\uparrow1}\frac{\Ecal_M(1;Q)}{(1-Q)^{A(M)}}
 =\prod_{m=1}^{M}m^{a_m}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{J}2^{jL_j}L_j!.
\end{equation}
Thus the finite determinant naturally defines a dyadic multiscale factorial.

\section{Mellin asymptotics of the $q$-Pochhammer tower}

Put $Q=\EulerE^{-t}$, $t>0$, and
\[
 y=\log\frac1t,
 \qquad
 u_t=\log_2\frac1t=\frac y{\log2}.
\]
The identity \cref{p7:eq:E-log} may be rewritten as
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E-Theta}
 -\log\Ecal(u;\EulerE^{-t})
 =\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{u^\ell}{\ell}\Theta(\ell t).
\end{equation}
This transfers the heat complex dimensions directly to a tower of $q$-Pochhammer symbols.

\subsection{The subcritical regime $|u|<1$}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:Pu}
 \Pcal_u(v)=\frac1{\log2}
 \sum_{k\ne0}\Gamma(\omega_k)\zeta(\omega_k)
 \Polylogarithm_{1+\omega_k}(u)\EulerE^{2\pi ikv}.
\end{equation}
For $u$ in a compact subset of the unit disk this series is absolutely convergent and analytic in both $u$ and $v$.

\begin{theorem}[Subcritical $q$-Euler asymptotic]\label{p7:thm:E-subcritical}
Fix $\rho<1$.  Uniformly for $|u|\le\rho$, every $J\ge0$, and every $0<\varepsilon<1$,
\begin{align}\label{p7:eq:E-subcritical-asymp}
 -\log\Ecal(u;\EulerE^{-t})
 ={}&\frac{2\Polylogarithm_2(u)}t
 +\frac{\log(1-u)}{2\log2}\,y
 +C(u)+\Pcal_u(u_t)\\
 &-\sum_{r=1}^{J}
 \frac{B_{2r}\Polylogarithm_{2-2r}(u)}{(2r)!(2^{2r-1}-1)}t^{2r-1}
 +O_{J,\rho,\varepsilon}(t^{2J+1-\varepsilon}),\notag
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:Cu}
 C(u)=-\frac1{2\log2}
 \left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Polylogarithm_s(u)\right|_{s=1}
 -C_\Theta\log(1-u).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $c>1$, Mellin inversion and absolute convergence give
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E-Mellin}
 -\log\Ecal(u;\EulerE^{-t})
 =\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}
 \Gamma(s)\Polylogarithm_{s+1}(u)\zeta_D(s)t^{-s}\ddPartxx s.
\end{equation}
At $s=1$ the residue is $2\Polylogarithm_2(u)/t$.  At $s=0$, write
\[
 \zeta_D(s)=\frac{a_{-1}}s+a_0+O(s),
 \quad
 a_{-1}=-\frac1{2\log2},
 \quad
 a_0=-\frac14-\frac{\log(2\pi)}{2\log2}.
\]
The double-pole residue after multiplication by $t^{-s}$ is
\[
 \frac{\log(1-u)}{2\log2}y
 -\frac1{2\log2}\left.\partial_s\Polylogarithm_s(u)\right|_{s=1}
 -C_\Theta\log(1-u).
\]
The poles $\omega_k$ give \cref{p7:eq:Pu}.  At $s=-(2r-1)$, the residue equals the displayed Bernoulli--polylogarithm coefficient.  At negative even integers the trivial zeros of $\zeta$ cancel the poles of $\Gamma$.
\end{proof}

The expansion is nonuniform at $u=1$: $\Polylogarithm_1(u)=-\log(1-u)$ diverges, and a second singular factor meets the pole at $s=0$.  The critical limit must be calculated separately.

\subsection{The critical product $u=1$}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PE}
 \Pcal_E(v)=\frac1{\log2}
 \sum_{k\ne0}\Gamma(\omega_k)\zeta(1+\omega_k)
 \zeta(\omega_k)\EulerE^{2\pi ikv}.
\end{equation}
Let $\gamma_1$ be the first Stieltjes constant in
\[
 \zeta(1+s)=\frac1s+\gamma-\gamma_1s+O(s^2).
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:CE}
 C_E=
 \frac{\zeta''(0)+\gamma_1+\gamma^2/2-\pi^2/12}{2\log2}
 -\frac{\log(2\pi)}4-\frac{\log2}{24}.
\end{equation}
Numerically, $C_E=-2.4612722039168654768\ldots$.

\begin{theorem}[Critical dyadic $q$-Pochhammer tower]\label{p7:thm:E-critical}
For every $N>0$,
\begin{align}\label{p7:eq:E-critical-asymp}
 -\log\Ecal(1;\EulerE^{-t})
 ={}&\frac{\pi^2}{3t}
 -\frac{y^2}{4\log2}
 -\left(\frac14+\frac{\log(2\pi)}{2\log2}\right)y\\
 &+C_E+\Pcal_E(u_t)+\frac{t}{24}+O_N(t^N)
 \qquad(t\downarrow0).\notag
\end{align}
Equivalently, this is the asymptotic of
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:E1-tower}
 \Ecal(1;\EulerE^{-t})
 =\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
 (\EulerE^{-2^jt};\EulerE^{-2^jt})_\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At $u=1$, \cref{p7:eq:E-Mellin} becomes
\[
 \frac1{2\pi i}\int
 \Gamma(s)\zeta(s+1)\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-2^{-s}}t^{-s}\ddPartxx s.
\]
The pole at $s=1$ contributes $2\zeta(2)/t=\pi^2/(3t)$.  At $s=0$ the three factors $\Gamma(s)$, $\zeta(s+1)$, and $(1-2^{-s})^{-1}$ produce a triple pole.  Write
\[
 \Gamma(s)\zeta(1+s)
 =\frac1{s^2}+H_0+O(s),
 \quad
 H_0=-\gamma_1-\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\frac{\pi^2}{12},
\]
and
\[
 \zeta_D(s)=\frac{a_{-1}}s+a_0+a_1s+O(s^2),
 \quad
 a_1=\frac{\zeta''(0)}{2\log2}
 -\frac{\log(2\pi)}4-\frac{\log2}{24}.
\]
The coefficient of $1/s$ after multiplication by $\EulerE^{sy}$ is
\[
 \frac{a_{-1}}2y^2+a_0y+a_1+a_{-1}H_0,
\]
which is exactly the quadratic-log part and $C_E$.  The poles $\omega_k$ give \cref{p7:eq:PE}.  The pole at $s=-1$ contributes $t/24$.  Every pole of $\Gamma$ at an integer $s=-n$, $n\ge2$, is canceled by a trivial zero of either $\zeta(s)$ or $\zeta(s+1)$.  The contour can therefore be moved arbitrarily far left, giving $O_N(t^N)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The quadratic logarithm is a critical phenomenon.  For $|u|<1$ the origin is a double Mellin pole and produces a term linear in $y$; at $u=1$, the pole of $\zeta(s+1)$ raises the order to three and produces $-y^2/(4\log2)$.
\end{remark}

\section{A symmetric $q$-integer deformation of the sinc product}

\subsection{Symmetric $q$-integers}

For $0<Q<1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-int}
 [m]_Q=\frac{Q^{-m/2}-Q^{m/2}}{Q^{-1/2}-Q^{1/2}}
 =Q^{-(m-1)/2}\frac{1-Q^m}{1-Q}.
\end{equation}
If $Q=\EulerE^{-t}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-int-sinh}
 [m]_Q=\frac{\sinh(mt/2)}{\sinh(t/2)}.
\end{equation}
Thus $[m]_Q\ge m$, $[m]_Q\to m$ as $Q\uparrow1$, and $[m]_Q$ grows exponentially in $m$ for fixed $Q<1$.  The multiplicative identity
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-int-mult}
 [ab]_Q=[a]_Q[b]_{Q^a}
\end{equation}
will encode exact $q$-self-similarity.

By functional calculus, let $[D]_Q$ be diagonal with eigenvalues $[2^jn]_Q$.

\begin{definition}[Symmetric dyadic $q$-sinc determinant]\label{p7:def:PhiQ}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ}
 \boxed{\ \Phi_Q(z)=\det(I-z^2[D]_Q^{-2})
 =\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}
 \left(1-\frac{z^2}{[m]_Q^2}\right)^{a_m}.\ }
\end{equation}
Also define the one-scale factor
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:SQ}
 S_Q(z)=\det(I-z^2[N]_Q^{-2})
 =\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{z^2}{[n]_Q^2}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}
Both are entire functions of order zero for fixed $Q<1$.

\subsection{Exact $q$-scaling}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-renormalization equation]\label{p7:thm:q-scaling}
The functional calculus version of $D\simeq N\oplus2D$ is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:qD-decomp}
 [D]_Q\simeq[N]_Q\oplus[2]_Q[D]_{Q^2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ-scale}
 \boxed{\ \Phi_Q(z)=S_Q(z)
 \Phi_{Q^2}\!\left(\frac{z}{[2]_Q}\right).\ }
\end{equation}
Iterating,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ-scale-iter}
 \boxed{\ \Phi_Q(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
 S_{Q^{2^j}}\!\left(\frac{z}{[2^j]_Q}\right).\ }
\end{equation}
As $Q\uparrow1$, this becomes the classical identity
\[
 \Phi(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\SincRad(\pi z/2^j).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On the $j\ge1$ subspace, the eigenvalues are $[2(2^{j-1}n)]_Q$.  By \cref{p7:eq:q-int-mult},
\[
 [2(2^{j-1}n)]_Q=[2]_Q[2^{j-1}n]_{Q^2}.
\]
This proves \cref{p7:eq:qD-decomp}.  Apply determinant multiplicativity to get \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-scale}; repeated use and
\[
 [2^j]_Q=\prod_{h=0}^{j-1}[2]_{Q^{2^h}}
\]
give \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-scale-iter}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact $q$-Pochhammer factorization}

Set
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:eta-lambda}
 \eta=\frac{z(1-Q)}{\sqrt Q},
 \qquad
 \lambda+\lambda^{-1}=2+\eta^2.
\end{equation}
Either root may be chosen; every formula below is symmetric under $\lambda\leftrightarrow\lambda^{-1}$.

\begin{theorem}[$q$-Pochhammer representation of $\Phi_Q$]\label{p7:thm:PhiQ-pch}
For every $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:single-qfactor}
 1-\frac{z^2}{[m]_Q^2}
 =\frac{(1-\lambda Q^m)(1-\lambda^{-1}Q^m)}{(1-Q^m)^2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ-E}
 \boxed{\ \Phi_Q(z)
 =\frac{\Ecal(\lambda;Q)\Ecal(\lambda^{-1};Q)}{\Ecal(1;Q)^2}\ }.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ-tower}
 \Phi_Q(z)=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(\lambda Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty
       (\lambda^{-1}Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty}
      {(Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty^2}.
\end{equation}
Likewise,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:SQ-pch}
 S_Q(z)=
 \frac{(\lambda Q;Q)_\infty(\lambda^{-1}Q;Q)_\infty}
      {(Q;Q)_\infty^2}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
From \cref{p7:eq:q-int},
\[
 \frac{z^2}{[m]_Q^2}
 =\frac{\eta^2Q^m}{(1-Q^m)^2}.
\]
The numerator after subtraction is
\[
 (1-Q^m)^2-\eta^2Q^m
 =1-(2+\eta^2)Q^m+Q^{2m}
 =(1-\lambda Q^m)(1-\lambda^{-1}Q^m),
\]
which proves \cref{p7:eq:single-qfactor}.  Raise to the multiplicity $a_m$, multiply over $m$, and use \cref{p7:eq:E-mult} and \cref{p7:eq:E-tower}.
\end{proof}

\begin{resultboxPartxx}{Direct bridge to the classical sinc product}
Combining \cref{p7:thm:PhiQ-pch} with the convergence theorem below gives the explicit limit
\[
 \Phi(z)=\lim_{Q\uparrow1}
 \prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
 \frac{(\lambda_Q(z)Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty
       (\lambda_Q(z)^{-1}Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty}
      {(Q^{2^j};Q^{2^j})_\infty^2},
\]
where $\lambda_Q(z)+\lambda_Q(z)^{-1}=2+z^2(1-Q)^2/Q$.  This is a direct $q$-Pochhammer representation of the infinite sinc product through a singular but controlled $Q\uparrow1$ limit.
\end{resultboxPartxx}

\subsection{Convergence to the Rvachev Fourier transform}

\begin{theorem}[Trace-norm and locally uniform limit]\label{p7:thm:PhiQ-limit}
As $Q\uparrow1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:trace-norm-limit}
 \bigl\|[D]_Q^{-2}-D^{-2}\bigr\|_1\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhiQ-limit}
 \boxed{\ \Phi_Q(z)\longrightarrow\Phi(z)\ }
\end{equation}
locally uniformly on $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The eigenvalues satisfy $[m]_Q\ge m$ and converge pointwise to $m$.  Hence
\[
 \bigl\|[D]_Q^{-2}-D^{-2}\bigr\|_1
 =\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_m
 \left(\frac1{m^2}-\frac1{[m]_Q^2}\right)
 \longrightarrow0
\]
by dominated convergence, dominated by $a_m/m^2$.  Continuity of the Fredholm determinant with respect to trace norm, uniformly when $z$ ranges over compact sets, proves the result.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact $q$-zero arithmetic and a Thue--Morse sign law}

\begin{theorem}[$q$-zero spectrum]\label{p7:thm:q-zeros}
For fixed $0<Q<1$, the nonzero zeros of $\Phi_Q$ are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-zero-set}
 z=\pm[m]_Q,\qquad m\ge1,
\end{equation}
with multiplicity $a_m=1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)$.  Hence
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-sign}
 \boxed{\ \sgnPartxx\Phi_Q(x)=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(M)}
 \quad\text{for}\quad [M]_Q<x<[M+1]_Q.\ }
\end{equation}
If $Q=\EulerE^{-t}$, define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:MQ}
 M_Q(R)=\Floor{
 \frac2t\arsinh\!\left(R\sinh\frac t2\right)
 }.
\end{equation}
Then the number of nonzero zeros in $|z|\le R$, counted with multiplicity, is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:q-zero-count}
 \boxed{\ \mathcal N_{\Phi_Q}(R)
 =4M_Q(R)-2\BinaryDigitSum(M_Q(R)).\ }
\end{equation}
For fixed $Q<1$, $\mathcal N_{\Phi_Q}(R)=O(\log R)$ and $\Phi_Q$ has order zero.  The limit $Q\uparrow1$ is a singular transition to the linear zero growth and order one of $\Phi$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The sequence $[m]_Q$ is strictly increasing.  The product \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ} gives the zero set and multiplicities.  Crossing the first $M$ positive zeros changes sign by $(-1)^{A(M)}=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(M)}$.  Formula \cref{p7:eq:q-int-sinh} shows that $[m]_Q\le R$ exactly when
\[
 m\le\frac2t\arsinh\!\left(R\sinh(t/2)\right),
\]
which proves the count.  Exponential growth of $[m]_Q$ gives logarithmic counting and order zero.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The $q$-spectral zeta and coefficient recurrence}

For $\Re s>0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:zetaDQ}
 \zeta_{D,Q}(s)=\TraceOperator[D]_Q^{-s}
 =\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_m}{[m]_Q^s}.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:A-lambert}
 \Acal(x)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_mx^m
 =\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{x^{2^j}}{1-x^{2^j}}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[$q$-spectral Lambert expansion]\label{p7:prop:zetaDQ}
For $\Re s>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:zetaDQ-Lambert}
 \boxed{\ \zeta_{D,Q}(s)
 =(1-Q)^sQ^{-s/2}
 \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(s)_k}{k!}
 \Acal\!\left(Q^{k+s/2}\right),\ }
\end{equation}
where $(s)_k=s(s+1)\cdots(s+k-1)$.  For $\Re s>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:zetaDQ-limit}
 \zeta_{D,Q}(s)\longrightarrow\zeta_D(s)
 \qquad(Q\uparrow1).
\end{equation}
If
\[
 \Phi_Q(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_n(Q)z^{2n},
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:cQ-rec}
 c_0(Q)=1,
 \qquad
 c_n(Q)=-\frac1n\sum_{r=1}^{n}
 \zeta_{D,Q}(2r)c_{n-r}(Q).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
From \cref{p7:eq:q-int},
\[
 [m]_Q^{-s}=(1-Q)^sQ^{-s/2}
 Q^{ms/2}(1-Q^m)^{-s}.
\]
Expand $(1-Q^m)^{-s}=\sum_{k\ge0}(s)_kQ^{mk}/k!$ and sum over $m$.  Dominated convergence using $[m]_Q\ge m$ proves \cref{p7:eq:zetaDQ-limit}.  Finally,
\[
 \log\Phi_Q(z)=-\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{\zeta_{D,Q}(2r)}r z^{2r}
 \qquad(|z|<1),
\]
and logarithmic differentiation gives \cref{p7:eq:cQ-rec}.
\end{proof}

\section{A base-$b$ extension}

The operator mechanism is not restricted to $2$.  Let $b\ge2$ be an integer and define
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:Db}
 D_b e_{j,n}=b^jn\,e_{j,n},
 \qquad
 \Phi_b(z)=\det(I-z^2D_b^{-2})
 =\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi z}{b^j}\right).
\end{equation}
Let $\nu_b(m)=\max\{j:b^j\mid m\}$ and $s_b(M)$ be the sum of the base-$b$ digits of $M$.

\begin{theorem}[Base-$b$ spectral package]\label{p7:thm:base-b}
One has
\begin{align}
 D_b&\simeq N\oplus bD_b,\label{p7:eq:Db-scale}\\
 \operatorname{mult}_{D_b}(m)&=1+\nu_b(m),\label{p7:eq:mb}\\
 \zeta_{D_b}(s)&=\frac{\zeta(s)}{1-b^{-s}},\label{p7:eq:zetab}\\
 A_b(M):=\sum_{m\le M}(1+\nu_b(m))
 &=\sum_{j\ge0}\Floor{\frac{M}{b^j}}
 =\frac{bM-s_b(M)}{b-1}.\label{p7:eq:Ab}
\end{align}
For symmetric $q$-integers,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhibQ}
 \Phi_{b,Q}(z)=\prod_{m\ge1}
 \left(1-\frac{z^2}{[m]_Q^2}\right)^{1+\nu_b(m)}
\end{equation}
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:PhibQ-scale}
 \Phi_{b,Q}(z)=S_Q(z)
 \Phi_{b,Q^b}\!\left(z/[b]_Q\right).
\end{equation}
The associated $q$-Euler determinant is
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:Eb}
 \Ecal_b(u;Q)=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
 (uQ^{b^j};Q^{b^j})_\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All statements follow by splitting off the $j=0$ summand and relabeling $j\mapsto j-1$.  The digit formula uses
\[
 \sum_{j\ge1}\Floor{\frac{M}{b^j}}
 =\frac{M-s_b(M)}{b-1}.
\]
The $q$-scaling follows from $[bn]_Q=[b]_Q[n]_{Q^b}$.
\end{proof}

The dyadic case is distinguished because the parity of \cref{p7:eq:Ab} collapses to
\[
 (-1)^{A_2(M)}=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(M)},
\]
which is exactly Thue--Morse.  Thus the Thue--Morse sign is not an accidental decoration: it is the parity character singled out by the base-$2$ spectral tower.

\section{Computational verification and reproducible checks}

\subsection{What was checked}

The following identities were independently evaluated at high precision:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
\item the leading zero-jet formula \cref{p7:eq:first-derivative-zero} for integers of several distinct $2$-adic valuations;
\item the factorization \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-E} by comparing the direct zero product and the quotient of dyadic $q$-Euler determinants;
\item convergence $\Phi_Q(z)\to\Phi(z)$ at real points inside and outside the first zero interval;
\item the heat expansion \cref{p7:eq:heat-asymp}, including the coefficient $-1/12$ of $t$ and the first Fourier coefficient of $\Pcal_\Theta$;
\item the critical expansion \cref{p7:eq:E-critical-asymp}, whose residual after the displayed $t/24$ term was below $6\times10^{-12}$ in the tested range $0.01\le t\le0.2$ with a finite Fourier truncation.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{$q\uparrow1$ sample values}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c|ccc@{}}
\toprule
$Q$ & $\Phi_Q(0.3)$ & $\Phi_Q(1.2)$ & $\Phi_Q(2.7)$\\
\midrule
0.80 & $0.8316534376$ & $-0.08646646$ & $-0.03017980$\\
0.90 & $0.8241751473$ & $-0.07432843$ & $-0.01331576$\\
0.95 & $0.8204894677$ & $-0.06907163$ & $-0.00902981$\\

0.99 & $0.8175541599$ & $-0.06518866$ & $-0.00669771$\\

The convergence is not claimed to be monotone in general; the table merely illustrates the trace-norm theorem.

\subsection{A practical evaluation hierarchy}

The new identities suggest several complementary algorithms:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \textbf{Near the origin:} use the coefficient recurrence \cref{p7:eq:c-rec} or its $q$-version \cref{p7:eq:cQ-rec}.
\item \textbf{Near an integer zero:} use the normalized local factor \cref{p7:eq:local-factor}; this removes catastrophic cancellation and exposes the exact multiplicity.
\item \textbf{On compact sets away from zeros:} use the finite canonical product with the certified tail estimate \cref{p7:eq:det-tail}.
\item \textbf{For $Q<1$:} use the $q$-Pochhammer quotient \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-tower}, exploiting fast exponential convergence at large scales.
\item \textbf{For $Q$ close to $1$:} combine the exact quotient with the critical asymptotic \cref{p7:eq:E-critical-asymp} to cancel large denominator terms analytically before numerical evaluation.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Connections back to the Fabius and Thue--Morse theories}

\subsection{Two independent appearances of $q$-binomial structure}

The existing Fabius corpus derives base-$1/2$ Gaussian-binomial coefficients from finite dyadic coefficient extraction and exact values of $F$ at dyadic rationals.  The present report derives Gaussian-binomial coefficients from a different object: finite spectral determinants of the zero divisor of $\FourierTwoPiTransformOf{\RvachevUp}$.  The common mechanism is multiscale dyadic refinement:
\[
 \text{dyadic value formulas}
 \longleftarrow \text{refinement blocks}
 \longrightarrow \text{spectral multiplicity blocks}.
\]
The two coefficient systems are not identical, but they live in the same category of products indexed by powers of $2$.  This opens a concrete comparison problem: identify transforms that carry the exact-value $q$-binomial arrays into the spectral arrays $C_{M,k}(Q)$ of \cref{p7:eq:CMk}.

\subsection{Thue--Morse as a determinant sign}

Equation \cref{p7:eq:spectral-parity} can be rewritten
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:tm-determinant-sign}
 \ThueMorseSignSymbol_M=\sgnPartxx\det\!\left(I-x^2D^{-2}\right)
 \qquad(M<x<M+1).
\end{equation}
Thus a binary automatic sequence is recovered from signs of a single entire Fredholm determinant.  The $q$-deformed version \cref{p7:eq:q-sign} moves the zero locations but preserves the automatic sign word exactly.

\subsection{Moments and zeros from one spectrum}

The same spectrum produces both:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=2em]
\item the zero multiplicities through its eigenvalue degeneracies;
\item the probability cumulants through the traces $\TraceOperator D^{-2r}$.
\end{itemize}
This is stronger than a formal analogy.  It says the local probability law of the Rvachev density and the global zero divisor of its Fourier transform are two evaluations of the same spectral data.

\subsection{Heat smoothing versus Fourier decay}

The active Fourier drafts study $\Phi(x)$ at large real $x$, where lacunary products create pointwise exceptional behavior and metric limit laws.  The heat trace $\Theta(t)$ studies the same multiplicities under exponential smoothing.  It is much more rigid: all oscillations are captured by the explicit vertical lattice $2\pi i\IntegerNumbers/\log2$.  A promising next step is to seek a transform connecting the log-periodic heat term to the periodic amplitudes that occur in endpoint asymptotics of $F$.

\section{Lean formalization roadmap}

The operator-theoretic presentation is conceptually compact, but a successful Lean formalization should proceed from finite identities to analytic limits.

\subsection{Layer 1: arithmetic and finite products}

The most accessible statements are:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
\item $a_m=1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)$ and the recursions $a_{2m}=a_m+1$, $a_{2m+1}=1$;
\item $\sum_{m\le M}a_m=2M-\BinaryDigitSum(M)$;
\item the finite factorization \cref{p7:eq:finite-pch};
\item the Gaussian-binomial coefficient formula \cref{p7:eq:CMk};
\item the finite form of the single-factor identity \cref{p7:eq:single-qfactor};
\item the base-$b$ digit identity \cref{p7:eq:Ab}.
\end{enumerate}
These require no infinite-dimensional analysis.

\subsection{Layer 2: locally finite zero factorization and cofactor jets}

This layer is now delivered.  The formal proof first establishes the total
shifted-sinc law, then the global denominator-cleared factorization at
\(2^kq\), and finally the canonical signed-integer form.  A local analytic
unit gives the exact complex order and the first nonzero derivative.  The
same formal layer now computes every derivative of order \(d_m+r\) from the
\(r\)-th derivative of the analytic cofactor \(U_m\).  The remaining
local-jet work is the separately normalized coefficient API: package \(R_m\),
identify it explicitly with \(U_m/U_m(0)\), and formalize its logarithmic and
Bell-polynomial derivatives and the literal coefficient-extraction notation
in \cref{p7:eq:all-jets}.

\subsection{Layer 3: infinite products and traces}

One can next formalize:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2em]
\item absolute convergence of $\sum_{j,n}(2^jn)^{-2}$;
\item the regrouped canonical product;
\item the Dirichlet-series identity $\zeta_D(s)=\zeta(s)/(1-2^{-s})$ on $\Re s>1$;
\item trace-norm convergence of the $q$-deformation, represented as convergence of summable diagonal sequences.
\end{enumerate}
Only after these are stable is it efficient to introduce Mathlib's operator determinant infrastructure, if the available API is sufficiently mature.

\subsection{Layer 4: Mellin asymptotics}

The heat and $q$-Euler expansions require contour shifting, residues, zeta continuation, and vertical estimates.  They are the deepest formalization target.  A useful intermediate milestone is a theorem schema: given a meromorphic Mellin transform with a finite specified pole set and suitable vertical decay, derive an asymptotic expansion.  The dyadic results would then become applications.

\section{Frontier questions}

\begin{problem}[Arithmetic of half-integer amplitudes]
Classify or sharply estimate the numbers $\Phi((2k+1)/2)$ that govern every leading integer-zero jet through \cref{p7:eq:Cm}.  Determine whether their signs and $2$-adic or transcendence properties admit formulae parallel to the exact dyadic values of $F$.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[Comparison of the two Gaussian-binomial webs]
Construct an explicit transform between the coefficient arrays in the exact dyadic-value formulas for $F$ and the spectral coefficients $C_{M,k}(Q)$ in \cref{p7:eq:CMk}.  The shared multiscale factorization suggests a common incidence algebra or a common family of refinement matrices.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[Critical double scaling]
Analyze $\Phi_Q(z)$ when $Q=\EulerE^{-t}\uparrow1$ and $z$ grows with $t^{-1}$.  The fixed-$Q$ function has order zero and logarithmic zero growth, while the limit has order one and linear zero growth.  Identify the transition kernels and scaling regimes.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[Heat periodicity and endpoint periodicity]
The heat trace has the explicit periodic wave \cref{p7:eq:Ptheta}; endpoint asymptotics of the Fabius function also contain periodic dependence on a dyadic logarithmic phase.  Determine whether both are projections of a single meromorphic two-variable transform.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[Twisted spectral traces]
Let $J e_{j,n}=\ThueMorseSignSymbol_n e_{j,n}$.  For $\Re s>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{p7:eq:twisted-zeta}
 \TraceOperator(JD^{-s})
 =\frac{1}{1-2^{-s}}
 \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\ThueMorseSignSymbol_n}{n^s}.
\end{equation}
Study its continuation, functional relations, and whether its singularities encode known Thue--Morse Dirichlet-series phenomena in a spectral form.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[Transfer operators]
Relate the diagonal spectral model $D$ to the transfer operators used in the Fourier-decay corpus.  The former captures the exact zero divisor and smoothed scale statistics; the latter captures real-axis orbit statistics.  A common operator category could unify zeros, decay, and probabilistic limit laws.
\end{problem}

\begin{conjecture}[Universality of the symmetric $q$-deformation]
Among deformations that (i) preserve the multiplicity word $1+\TwoAdicValuation(m)$, (ii) obey an exact two-scale factorization, (iii) have order zero for fixed $Q<1$, and (iv) converge locally uniformly to $\Phi$, the symmetric $q$-integer model \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ} is unique up to an analytic reparameterization of $Q$ and a scalar rescaling of $z$.
\end{conjecture}

\section{Conclusion}

The infinite sinc product of Rvachev's up-function admits a spectral organization that is invisible when the product is treated only factor by factor.  The diagonal operator $D$ turns dyadic refinement into the unitary decomposition $D\simeq N\oplus2D$.  Its eigenvalue degeneracies are the integer-zero multiplicities; its finite spectral parity is the Thue--Morse sequence; its zeta function is $\zeta(s)/(1-2^{-s})$; its complex dimensions generate a log-periodic heat wave; and its power traces recover the probability cumulants.

The same operator admits two complementary $q$-constructions.  The determinant $\Ecal(u;Q)=\det(I-uQ^D)$ is a dyadic tower of $q$-Pochhammer symbols with exact finite Gaussian-binomial coefficients and explicit Mellin asymptotics.  Replacing each spectral integer by its symmetric $q$-integer produces $\Phi_Q$, an order-zero entire function with the same multiplicity and Thue--Morse sign word, an exact $q$-renormalization equation, and a locally uniform limit to the Rvachev Fourier transform.  Formula \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-tower} is the central new bridge: it connects $q$-Pochhammer symbols directly to the zero divisor of an infinite sinc product, while \cref{p7:eq:PhiQ-scale} shows that the connection is dictated by self-similarity rather than by a formal change of notation.

The resulting program is concrete.  Its finite arithmetic core, total local
integer-zero factorization, exact complex order, lower vanishings, and every
higher jet expressed through derivatives of the analytic cofactor are now
machine checked.  The separately normalized \(R_m\) coefficient, logarithmic,
and Bell-polynomial packages remain frontier work; the
Mellin theory supplies exact human-readable asymptotics, and its unresolved
comparison with the exact dyadic-value $q$-calculus offers a sharply defined
next frontier.

\section{Residue calculations for the critical $q$-Euler product}\label{p7:app:residues}

For reference, the local series used in \cref{p7:thm:E-critical} are
\begin{align*}
 \Gamma(s)&=\frac1s-\gamma+
 \left(\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\frac{\pi^2}{12}\right)s+O(s^2),\\
 \zeta(1+s)&=\frac1s+\gamma-\gamma_1s+O(s^2),\\
 \frac1{1-\EulerE^{-Ls}}&=\frac1{Ls}+\frac12+\frac{Ls}{12}+O(s^3),\\
 \zeta(s)&=-\frac12-\frac{\log(2\pi)}2s+\frac{\zeta''(0)}2s^2+O(s^3).
\end{align*}
Hence
\begin{align*}
 \Gamma(s)\zeta(1+s)
 &=\frac1{s^2}+
 \left(-\gamma_1-\frac{\gamma^2}{2}+\frac{\pi^2}{12}\right)+O(s),\\
 \frac{\zeta(s)}{1-\EulerE^{-Ls}}
 &=-\frac1{2L}\frac1s
 -\frac14-\frac{\log(2\pi)}{2L}\\
 &\quad+
 \left(\frac{\zeta''(0)}{2L}
 -\frac{\log(2\pi)}4-\frac L{24}\right)s+O(s^2).
\end{align*}
Multiplication by $t^{-s}=\EulerE^{sy}$ then yields the $y^2$, $y$, and constant terms in \cref{p7:eq:E-critical-asymp}.

\frontierreportpart{Logarithmic q-Richardson Acceleration and Lambert Phase
Locking in Fabius--Rvachev Analysis}
% Absorbed from fabius_frontier_new_results/fabius_frontier_new_results.tex


% Standalone front-matter page resets are suppressed in the collected volume.
\clearpage

\begin{abstract}
The Fabius function, Rvachev's compactly supported $C^\infty$ up-function, the signed Thue--Morse sequence, and dyadic $q$-calculus form a tightly connected system. The existing \repoPartll{} corpus already contains extensive exact dyadic arithmetic, Gaussian-binomial interpolation, moment and cumulant formulas involving Bernoulli and Bell polynomials, Fourier products, Lambert-$W$ endpoint asymptotics with a nonconstant periodic factor, and several forms of Richardson extrapolation. After a repository-wide audit of the living documents, frozen historical versions, research drafts, and bundled source papers, this report develops two extrapolation mechanisms that are not stated there.

First, for the generalized lacunary sinc product
\[
P_b(t)=\prod_{k\ge 1}\SincRad(t/b^k),\qquad b>1,
\]
we obtain an exact Bernoulli--zeta expansion of the omitted logarithmic tail. Applying Lagrange interpolation to the logarithms of the truncations at the geometric nodes $q^j$, $q=b^{-2}$, gives a multiplicative Richardson transform with closed Gaussian-$q$-binomial weights. Its full error series is exact, has a fixed sign $(-1)^r$ at level $r$, yields consecutive certified upper and lower bounds, and admits an explicit nonasymptotic remainder estimate. The $\ell^1$ norm of the weights is exactly $(-q;q)_r/(q;q)_r$, hence remains bounded as the order tends to infinity; in the dyadic case it stays below $1.970$. The method therefore combines high order with unusually mild conditioning. Bell polynomials convert the logarithmic error into relative-error and derivative formulas, while a finite Thue--Morse exponential-sum identity transfers the accelerated product to purely finite signed digital sums.

Second, the exact inverse coordinate
\[
\lambda(x)=-\frac{1}{\log 2}W_{-1}(-x\log 2),\qquad x=\lambda 2^{-\lambda},
\]
is used to build phase-locked nodes $x_j=(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)}$. Periodic coefficient functions are identical at all these nodes. Ordinary Lagrange extrapolation in $h_j=(\lambda+j)^{-1}$ then cancels the first $r$ inverse-$\lambda$ corrections and extracts the periodic endpoint term $\Psi(\lambda)$ directly from values of $F$. The exact weights, all transformed moments, and their complete-homogeneous/Bell-polynomial representation are derived. Bernoulli polynomials describe the arithmetic-node power sums needed in conditioning and preconditioning. Together these results provide a new bridge between $q$-binomial interpolation, sinc products, Thue--Morse blocks, Lambert-$W$ phase geometry, Bernoulli coefficients, Bell-polynomial reconstruction, and Richardson extrapolation.
\end{abstract}


\clearpage

\section{Scope, corpus audit, and nonduplication boundary}

\subsection{The audited corpus}

The target tree contains living expositions, a deliberately quarantined frontier volume, active drafts, frozen historical versions, and source-paper transcriptions. The audit used the current tree and the archive ledger as of 27 August 2026. Counting each distinct \texttt{.tex} path, the inventory contains 57 sources: 25 in the living tree and 32 frozen historical sources. The living set consists of four top-level expositions, the canonical frontier volume, one Thue--Morse atlas, twelve Fourier-decay dossiers, and seven source-paper transcriptions.

\begin{table}[htbp]

\label{p8:tab:inventory}

\toprule
Family & TeX files & Principal content relevant to this report \\
\midrule
Living primary expositions & 4 & Fabius/up synthesis, glossary, non-elementarity, Lean walkthrough \\
Canonical frontier volume & 1 & Dyadic web, repeated integration, endpoint asymptotics, Thue--Morse convergence, saddle analysis \\
Living formula atlas & 1 & Thue--Morse identities, Prouhet cancellation, rarefaction, Mahler structure, Bell/Bernoulli formulas \\
Living Fourier-decay dossiers & 12 & Infinite sinc products, zeros, dyadic renormalization, envelopes, Lambert-$W$ scales, competing asymptotic formulations \\
Bundled source-paper TeX & 7 & Lacunary products, up-function background, arithmetic of $F$, approximation literature \\

The archive matters for originality. Several ideas no longer visible in the living primary paper survive in an older parallel draft or a superseded dossier. In particular, the audit treated the following as prior art rather than rediscoveries:
\begin{itemize}
\item Gaussian-$q$-binomial coefficient extraction at geometric nodes;
\item exact dyadic Richardson transforms for sequences modeled by powers of $2^{-p}$;
\item Bernoulli-number cumulants and Bell-polynomial moment reconstruction;
\item Prouhet cancellation for signed Thue--Morse blocks;
\item fixed-numerator Lambert phase locking along dyadic endpoint rays;
\item the periodic factor in the corrected small-argument asymptotic;
\item exact finite Thue--Morse products and Bromwich/coefficient representations;
\item global Fourier-decay envelopes and the zero geometry of the up transform.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{What is new relative to the repository}

The exact constructions below were not found in any audited source:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Richardson extrapolation of the \emph{logarithm} of truncated lacunary sinc products, followed by exponentiation to a multiplicative approximation;
\item the exact Gaussian-$q$-binomial formula for those log-extrapolation weights together with the full transformed Bernoulli--zeta tail;
\item the sign theorem and resulting alternating certified brackets for the infinite product;
\item the exact bounded condition number $(-q;q)_r/(q;q)_r$ of the level-$r$ log transform;
\item the Bell-polynomial relative-error and derivative hierarchy of the accelerated product;
\item transfer of this accelerated approximation to finite signed Thue--Morse exponential sums;
\item Lambert phase-locked Richardson nodes $x_j=(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)}$ designed specifically to extract the periodic endpoint term $\Psi$;
\item exact transformed inverse-power moments on that lattice, including their complete-homogeneous and Bell-polynomial form.
\end{enumerate}

A targeted literature search located general Richardson-extrapolation theory and the standard ingredients separately, but no exact counterpart of the combined sinc-tail construction. The responsible statement is therefore ``new relative to the audited repository and apparently not standard,'' not a claim of global priority.

\subsection{Proof-status convention}

Every substantial assertion is tagged implicitly by its placement:
\begin{itemize}
\item Results in Sections~\ref{p8:sec:tail}--\ref{p8:sec:thue-morse-transfer} are \statusprovedPartll.
\item The logarithmic phase-locked Lambert transform in
Section~\ref{p8:sec:lambert} is \statusprovedPartll{} and Lean-backed at every
fixed extrapolation order and residual depth.  Its all-orders endpoint input is
inherited from the formally verified endpoint theory, and the compiled
pullback, growing-weight, and finite-residual layers now discharge the
uniformity step.
\item Items in Section~\ref{p8:sec:open} are explicitly conjectural or open.
\end{itemize}
Here ``proved'' means proved in this mathematical report, not necessarily formalized in
Lean.  In particular, the endpoint input is represented by
\texttt{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient},
\path{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertMain_hasAsymptoticExpansion}, and
\path{log_fabius_sub_sharpLambertExpansion_isBigO} in the Lean development,
while the logarithmic phase-extraction transform is implemented by
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseLockedPullback.lean},
\path{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean},
\path{LambertReciprocalAsymptotics.lean}, and
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtraction.lean}.  The finite
complete-homogeneous/Bell/generalized-harmonic conversion and the exact
Bell rewrites of each residual term and finite residual partial sum are
implemented by \path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean},
\path{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean}, and
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtractionBell.lean}.  This closed status does not
extend to convergence of an infinite residual series, the exponentiated
estimator's Bell relative-error hierarchy, the higher derivative extractors,
a Lambert sign/bracketing theorem, or estimates uniform in growing
extrapolation order.  The remaining sinc-tail sign, bracketing, and
quantitative analytic claims are separate formalization obligations.

\section{Notation and structural background}
\label{p8:sec:notation}

\subsection{Sinc products and the Rvachev transform}

We use the entire normalized sinc function
\[
\SincRad z=\begin{cases}\sin z/z,&z\ne0,\\1,&z=0.\end{cases}
\]
For $b>1$, define
\begin{equation}
P_b(t)=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{b^k}\right),
\qquad
P_{b,N}(t)=\prod_{k=1}^{N}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{t}{b^k}\right).
\label{p8:eq:Pb-def}
\end{equation}
The dyadic case is $P=P_2$. In a common Fourier normalization for Rvachev's up-function,
\begin{equation}
\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)=\prod_{h=0}^{\infty}\SincRad\!\left(\frac{\pi\xi}{2^h}\right)
=\SincRad(\pi\xi)P_2(\pi\xi).
\label{p8:eq:up-factor}
\end{equation}
Thus every result for $P_2$ immediately applies to the nontrivial tail of $\FourierTwoPiTransformOf{\RvachevUp}$.

Set
\begin{equation}
q=b^{-2}\in(0,1),\qquad z_N=q^{N+1},\qquad
\rho_N=\frac{|t|}{b^{N+1}}=|t|z_N^{1/2}.
\label{p8:eq:q-z-rho}
\end{equation}
The local analysis below requires $\rho_N<\pi$, exactly the condition that every omitted sinc factor lies before its first nonzero zero.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-notation}

For $r\in\NPartll$,
\[
(a;q)_r=\prod_{k=0}^{r-1}(1-aq^k),\qquad
(q;q)_r=\prod_{k=1}^{r}(1-q^k),
\]
and
\[
\GaussianBinomial{r}{j}{q}=\frac{(q;q)_r}{(q;q)_j(q;q)_{r-j}}
\quad(0\le j\le r).
\]
We will repeatedly use the finite $q$-binomial theorem
\begin{equation}
\prod_{k=0}^{r-1}(1+zq^k)
=\sum_{j=0}^{r}q^{\binom{j}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{r}{j}{q} z^j.
\label{p8:eq:q-binomial}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Bernoulli and Bell polynomials}

The Bernoulli polynomials are defined by
\begin{equation}
\frac{te^{xt}}{e^t-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}B_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!},
\qquad B_n=B_n(0).
\label{p8:eq:bernoulli-gen}
\end{equation}
The complete exponential Bell polynomials $\mathcal B_n$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
\exp\!\left(\sum_{m\ge1}x_m\frac{u^m}{m!}\right)
=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathcal B_n(x_1,\ldots,x_n)\frac{u^n}{n!}.
\label{p8:eq:bell-gen}
\end{equation}
They are the natural conversion device from logarithmic/cumulant data to multiplicative/moment data.

\subsection{The lower-Lambert coordinate}

Let $L=\log 2$. For sufficiently small $x>0$, set
\begin{equation}
\lambda(x)=-\frac{1}{L}W_{-1}(-Lx).
\label{p8:eq:lambda-def}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
x=\lambda 2^{-\lambda},\qquad \lambda>\frac1L.
\label{p8:eq:lambda-inverse}
\end{equation}
The lower branch is essential: on $\lambda>1/L$, the map $\lambda\mapsto\lambda2^{-\lambda}$ is strictly decreasing and hence invertible.

\section{Exact logarithmic tails and multiplicative \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Richardson acceleration}

\section{The exact Bernoulli--zeta tail of a lacunary sinc product}
\label{p8:sec:tail}

\subsection{The logarithmic sinc series}

\begin{lemma}[Bernoulli--zeta expansion]
\label{p8:lem:logsinc}
For $|u|<\pi$,
\begin{equation}
\log\frac{\sin u}{u}
=-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\gamma_m u^{2m},
\qquad
\gamma_m=\frac{\zeta(2m)}{m\pi^{2m}}
=\frac{2^{2m-1}|B_{2m}|}{m(2m)!}.
\label{p8:eq:logsinc}
\end{equation}
Every $\gamma_m$ is strictly positive.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Euler's product
\(
\sin u/u=\prod_{n\ge1}(1-u^2/(\pi^2n^2))
\)
is locally uniformly convergent for $|u|<\pi$. Expanding $\log(1-v)=-\sum_{m\ge1}v^m/m$ and interchanging the absolutely convergent sums gives
\[
\log\frac{\sin u}{u}
=-\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{u^{2m}}{m\pi^{2m}}
\sum_{n\ge1}n^{-2m}.
\]
The Bernoulli form follows from Euler's evaluation of $\zeta(2m)$.
\end{proof}

The first coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\log\SincRad u=-\frac{u^2}{6}-\frac{u^4}{180}
-\frac{u^6}{2835}-\frac{u^8}{37800}-\cdots.
\label{p8:eq:logsinc-first}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Exact omitted-tail formula}

\begin{theorem}[Exact logarithmic tail]
\label{p8:thm:exact-tail}
Let $b>1$, $N\ge0$, and $t\in\RPartll$ satisfy $\rho_N<\pi$. If $P_{b,N}(t)\ne0$, then $P_b(t)$ and $P_{b,N}(t)$ have the same sign and
\begin{equation}
\log|P_{b,N}(t)|-\log|P_b(t)|
=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}d_m(t)z_N^m,
\label{p8:eq:tail-z}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
d_m(t)=\frac{\gamma_m t^{2m}}{1-q^m}
=\frac{\zeta(2m)t^{2m}}{m\pi^{2m}(1-q^m)}.
\label{p8:eq:dm}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
\log|P_{b,N}(t)|-\log|P_b(t)|
=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}
\frac{\zeta(2m)}{m\pi^{2m}(1-q^m)}\rho_N^{2m}.
\label{p8:eq:tail-rho}
\end{equation}
The series is absolutely convergent and strictly positive for $t\ne0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $k\ge N+1$ we have $|t|/b^k\le\rho_N<\pi$, so every omitted sinc factor is positive.
Moreover, the logarithms of the omitted factors are absolutely summable by
\cref{p8:lem:logsinc}; their product therefore converges to a strictly positive, nonzero
limit.  Hence truncation and limit have the same sign.  Applying the same expansion and
using absolute convergence,
\begin{align*}
\log|P_b(t)|-\log|P_{b,N}(t)|
&=\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\log\SincRad(t/b^k)\\
&=-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\gamma_m t^{2m}
\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}b^{-2mk}\\
&=-\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\gamma_m t^{2m}
\frac{q^{m(N+1)}}{1-q^m}.
\end{align*}
Negating yields the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Analytic Bernoulli correction]
\label{p8:cor:analytic-correction}
For $M\ge0$, define
\begin{equation}
C_{b,N}^{[M]}(t)=P_{b,N}(t)
\exp\!\left(-\sum_{m=1}^{M}d_m(t)z_N^m\right).
\label{p8:eq:analytic-corrected}
\end{equation}
Then $C_{b,N}^{[M]}(t)$ has the same sign as $P_b(t)$ and
\begin{equation}
\log\frac{|C_{b,N}^{[M]}(t)|}{|P_b(t)|}
=\sum_{m=M+1}^{\infty}d_m(t)z_N^m>0
\quad(t\ne0).
\label{p8:eq:analytic-correction-error}
\end{equation}
Thus $|C_{b,N}^{[M]}(t)|$ is a rigorous upper bound for $|P_b(t)|$.
\end{corollary}

This correction is explicit but uses $\zeta(2m)$ or Bernoulli numbers. The next construction achieves cancellation from product values alone.

\section{Geometric Lagrange weights and their \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-binomial form}
\label{p8:sec:qweights}

\subsection{Interpolation at \texorpdfstring{$1,q,\ldots,q^r$}{1, q, ..., q-to-the-r}}

For an integer $r\ge0$, let $\lambda_{r,j}(q)$ be the Lagrange basis weight for evaluation at $0$ using nodes $q^0,\ldots,q^r$:
\begin{equation}
\lambda_{r,j}(q)=\prod_{\substack{0\le k\le r\\k\ne j}}
\frac{-q^k}{q^j-q^k}.
\label{p8:eq:lambda-def-q}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian-$q$-binomial weights]
\label{p8:thm:qweights}
For $0\le j\le r$,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\lambda_{r,j}(q)
=\frac{(-1)^{r-j}q^{\binom{r-j+1}{2}}}
{(q;q)_j(q;q)_{r-j}}
=\frac{(-1)^{r-j}q^{\binom{r-j+1}{2}}}{(q;q)_r}
\GaussianBinomial{r}{j}{q}.}
\label{p8:eq:qweights}
\end{equation}
Their power moments are
\begin{equation}
S_{r,m}(q):=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}(q)q^{jm}
=\frac{q^{rm}(q^{1-m};q)_r}{(q;q)_r}.
\label{p8:eq:qmoment-full}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
S_{r,0}=1,
\qquad S_{r,m}=0\quad(1\le m\le r),
\label{p8:eq:qmoment-cancel}
\end{equation}
and, for $m\ge r+1$,
\begin{equation}
S_{r,m}(q)
=(-1)^r q^{r(r+1)/2}
\frac{(q^{m-r};q)_r}{(q;q)_r}.
\label{p8:eq:qmoment-positive}
\end{equation}
In particular, every nonzero residual moment has sign $(-1)^r$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Splitting the product in \cref{p8:eq:lambda-def-q} into $k<j$ and $k>j$ gives \cref{p8:eq:qweights}. For the moments, substitute the Gaussian-binomial form and change variables $k=r-j$. The finite $q$-binomial theorem \cref{p8:eq:q-binomial}, with a suitable monomial factor extracted, gives \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-full}. The zeros for $1\le m\le r$ arise because $(q^{1-m};q)_r$ contains the factor $1-q^0$. For $m\ge r+1$, reverse the negative powers in the product to obtain \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-positive}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An exact condition number}

The alternating weights might appear dangerous, but their total variation is exceptionally well behaved.

\begin{theorem}[Bounded $\ell^1$ norm]
\label{p8:thm:qcondition}
The absolute weight sum is
\begin{equation}
\kappa_r(q):=\sum_{j=0}^{r}|\lambda_{r,j}(q)|
=\frac{(-q;q)_r}{(q;q)_r}
=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\frac{1+q^k}{1-q^k}.
\label{p8:eq:qcondition}
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
\kappa_r(q)\nearrow \kappa_\infty(q)
:=\frac{(-q;q)_\infty}{(q;q)_\infty}<\infty.
\label{p8:eq:qcondition-limit}
\end{equation}
For $q=1/4$,
\begin{equation}
\kappa_\infty(1/4)=1.9692603536682694319\ldots.
\label{p8:eq:qcondition-dyadic}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Set $k=r-j$ in the absolute-value sum and use \cref{p8:eq:qweights}:
\[
\kappa_r(q)=\frac1{(q;q)_r}
\sum_{k=0}^{r}q^{k(k+1)/2}\GaussianBinomial{r}{k}{q}.
\]
\Cref{p8:eq:q-binomial} with $z=q$ identifies the numerator as
$\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(1+q^{i+1})=(-q;q)_r$. The infinite product converges because $\sum q^k<\infty$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This boundedness is not generic for high-order polynomial extrapolation. It is a special consequence of the geometric nodes and is one reason to extrapolate the logarithms: the inputs are of comparable scale, while the transformation itself has uniformly bounded absolute sensitivity.
\end{remark}

\section{Multiplicative \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-Richardson extrapolation}
\label{p8:sec:qrichardson}

\subsection{Definition and exact transformed error}

Assume $\rho_N<\pi$ and $P_{b,N}(t)\ne0$. Define
\begin{equation}
\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)
=\sgnPartll(P_{b,N}(t))
\exp\!\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}(q)
\log|P_{b,N+j}(t)|\right).
\label{p8:eq:Phat}
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)|
=\prod_{j=0}^{r}|P_{b,N+j}(t)|^{\lambda_{r,j}(q)}.
\label{p8:eq:Phat-product}
\end{equation}
Negative exponents are harmless because the relevant truncations are nonzero.

\begin{theorem}[Exact multiplicative extrapolation error]
\label{p8:thm:exact-rich-error}
Under the preceding hypotheses,
\begin{align}
E_{b;N,r}(t)
&:=\log\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)|}{|P_b(t)|}
\label{p8:eq:E-def}\\
&=\sum_{m=r+1}^{\infty}d_m(t)z_N^mS_{r,m}(q)
\label{p8:eq:E-series}\\
&=\frac{(-1)^rq^{r(r+1)/2}}{(q;q)_r}
\sum_{m=r+1}^{\infty}
\frac{\zeta(2m)}{m\pi^{2m}}
\frac{(q^{m-r};q)_r}{1-q^m}\rho_N^{2m}.
\label{p8:eq:E-explicit}
\end{align}
The series is absolutely convergent. If $t\ne0$, then
\begin{equation}
\sgnPartll E_{b;N,r}(t)=(-1)^r.
\label{p8:eq:E-sign}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{p8:thm:exact-tail} to each truncation $N+j$:
\[
\log|P_{b,N+j}|=\log|P_b|+
\sum_{m\ge1}d_m z_N^m q^{jm}.
\]
The constant term survives because $S_{r,0}=1$, while the first $r$ powers vanish by \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-cancel}. This proves \cref{p8:eq:E-series}. \Cref{p8:eq:E-explicit} follows from \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-positive}. Every factor in its sum is positive, proving the sign statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Certified alternating brackets}

\begin{corollary}[Rigorous upper and lower bounds]
\label{p8:cor:brackets}
For $t\ne0$ under the hypotheses of \cref{p8:thm:exact-rich-error},
\begin{equation}
|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{2s+1}(t)|<|P_b(t)|<
|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{2s}(t)|
\qquad(s\ge0).
\label{p8:eq:bracket}
\end{equation}
Thus any two consecutive extrapolation levels give a certified enclosure of the infinite product. In particular,
\begin{equation}
\left|\log\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)|}{|P_b(t)|}\right|
\le
\left|\log\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)|}
{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r+1}(t)|}\right|
\label{p8:eq:aposteriori-log}
\end{equation}
whenever the two levels lie on opposite sides as in \cref{p8:eq:bracket}.
\end{corollary}

The bracket is stronger than a mere asymptotic claim: it follows from the exact sign of every term in the transformed tail.

\subsection{Leading term and explicit remainder bound}

\begin{corollary}[Sharp leading error]
\label{p8:cor:leading-error}
As $\rho_N\to0$ with $r$ fixed,
\begin{equation}
E_{b;N,r}(t)
=C_r(q)\rho_N^{2r+2}+O(\rho_N^{2r+4}),
\label{p8:eq:leading-error}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
C_r(q)=(-1)^r q^{r(r+1)/2}
\frac{\zeta(2r+2)}{(r+1)\pi^{2r+2}(1-q^{r+1})}.
\label{p8:eq:Cr}
\end{equation}
For the dyadic case $q=1/4$,
\begin{equation}
C_0=\frac29,\qquad
C_1=-\frac1{675},\qquad
C_2=\frac1{178605},\qquad
C_3=-\frac1{154224000}.
\label{p8:eq:Cr-dyadic}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}[Nonasymptotic error bound]
\label{p8:thm:error-bound}
Let $a=(\rho_N/\pi)^2<1$. Then
\begin{equation}
|E_{b;N,r}(t)|
\le
\frac{q^{r(r+1)/2}\zeta(2)}
{(q;q)_r(1-q)(r+1)}
\frac{a^{r+1}}{1-a}.
\label{p8:eq:error-bound}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every fixed admissible pair $(t,N)$,
\begin{equation}
\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)\longrightarrow P_b(t)
\qquad(r\to\infty)
\label{p8:eq:r-convergence}
\end{equation}
with a supergeometric factor $q^{r(r+1)/2}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $m\ge r+1$, \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-positive} gives
\[
|S_{r,m}(q)|\le\frac{q^{r(r+1)/2}}{(q;q)_r}.
\]
Also $\zeta(2m)\le\zeta(2)$, $m^{-1}\le(r+1)^{-1}$, and $(1-q^m)^{-1}\le(1-q)^{-1}$. Sum the resulting geometric series in $a$. Since $(q;q)_r$ tends to the positive limit $(q;q)_\infty$, the right-hand side tends to zero faster than $q^{r^2/2}$ times an exponential factor.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Richardson table and multiplicative recurrence}

The transform can be evaluated without forming the closed weights.

\begin{algorithm}[Logarithmic $q$-Richardson table]
\label{p8:alg:rich-table}
Set
\[
R_{N,0}(t)=\log|P_{b,N}(t)|.
\]
For $s\ge1$, recursively define
\begin{equation}
R_{N,s}(t)=
\frac{R_{N+1,s-1}(t)-q^sR_{N,s-1}(t)}{1-q^s}.
\label{p8:eq:rich-recursion}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
R_{N,r}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}(q)
\log|P_{b,N+j}(t)|,
\qquad
\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}=\sgnPartll(P_{b,N})e^{R_{N,r}}.
\label{p8:eq:rich-table-result}
\end{equation}
\end{algorithm}

In multiplicative form,
\begin{equation}
|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{s}|
=\left(
\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N+1}{s-1}|}
{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{s-1}|^{q^s}}
\right)^{1/(1-q^s)}.
\label{p8:eq:multiplicative-recursion}
\end{equation}
The logarithmic implementation is preferable because it avoids underflow in the rapidly decaying Fourier regime.

\subsection{Explicit dyadic levels}

For $b=2$, $q=1/4$. The first transforms are
\begin{align}
|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{1}|
&=|P_N|^{-1/3}|P_{N+1}|^{4/3},
\label{p8:eq:r1-dyadic}\\
|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{2}|
&=|P_N|^{1/45}|P_{N+1}|^{-4/9}|P_{N+2}|^{64/45},
\label{p8:eq:r2-dyadic}\\
|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{3}|
&=|P_N|^{-1/2835}|P_{N+1}|^{4/135}
|P_{N+2}|^{-64/135}|P_{N+3}|^{4096/2835}.
\label{p8:eq:r3-dyadic}
\end{align}
The log errors begin respectively at orders $\rho_N^4$, $\rho_N^6$, and $\rho_N^8$.

\subsection{Hybrid Bernoulli--Richardson correction}

The exact tail coefficients and the data-driven transform can be combined.

\begin{corollary}[Hybrid correction]
\label{p8:cor:hybrid}
For $s\ge0$, define
\begin{equation}
\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}^{[s]}(t)
=\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)
\exp\!\left(-\sum_{m=r+1}^{r+s}
 d_m(t)z_N^mS_{r,m}(q)\right).
\label{p8:eq:hybrid}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\log\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}^{[s]}(t)|}{|P_b(t)|}
=\sum_{m=r+s+1}^{\infty}d_m(t)z_N^mS_{r,m}(q),
\label{p8:eq:hybrid-error}
\end{equation}
which still has sign $(-1)^r$. Thus the hybrid retains certified bracketing while moving the first nonzero term from degree $r+1$ to degree $r+s+1$.
\end{corollary}

This is the most direct point at which Bernoulli numbers and Gaussian $q$-binomial weights cooperate in one exact numerical scheme.

\section{Bell-polynomial error calculus and derivatives}
\label{p8:sec:bell}

\subsection{From logarithmic error to relative error}

Write
\begin{equation}
e_m^{(r)}(t)=d_m(t)S_{r,m}(q),
\qquad e_m^{(r)}=0\quad(1\le m\le r).
\label{p8:eq:em}
\end{equation}
Then \cref{p8:eq:E-series} is $E=\sum_{m\ge1}e_m^{(r)}z_N^m$. Exponentiation and \cref{p8:eq:bell-gen} give an exact convergent expansion.

\begin{theorem}[Bell-polynomial relative-error expansion]
\label{p8:thm:bell-relative}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{p8:thm:exact-rich-error},
\begin{equation}
\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}(t)|}{|P_b(t)|}
=1+\sum_{n=r+1}^{\infty}
\frac{1}{n!}
\mathcal B_n\!\left(1!e_1^{(r)},2!e_2^{(r)},\ldots,n!e_n^{(r)}\right)z_N^n.
\label{p8:eq:bell-relative}
\end{equation}
In particular, because $e_1^{(r)}=\cdots=e_r^{(r)}=0$, the first relative-error coefficient equals the first logarithmic-error coefficient:
\begin{equation}
\frac{|\GeometricProductExtrapolant{b}{N}{r}|}{|P_b|}-1
=e_{r+1}^{(r)}z_N^{r+1}+O(z_N^{r+2}).
\label{p8:eq:first-relative}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The next coefficients exhibit the familiar cumulant-to-moment pattern. For example,
\begin{align*}
[z_N^{r+2}]\left(e^E-1\right)&=e_{r+2}^{(r)}
&& (r\ge1),\\
[z_N^{2r+2}]\left(e^E-1\right)&=e_{2r+2}^{(r)}+\frac12(e_{r+1}^{(r)})^2.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Logarithmic derivatives}

On an interval avoiding zeros, differentiation of \cref{p8:eq:tail-z} is legitimate termwise. Let
\[
D^\ell=\frac{\ddPartll^\ell}{\ddPartll t^\ell},\qquad
(2m)_{\ell}=2m(2m-1)\cdots(2m-\ell+1).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Derivative acceleration]
\label{p8:thm:derivative-accel}
For every fixed $\ell\ge0$ and admissible $t$ away from zeros,
\begin{equation}
D^\ell\!\left(
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}\log|P_{b,N+j}(t)|
-\log|P_b(t)|\right)
=
\sum_{m\ge\max(r+1,\Ceiling{\ell/2})}
\frac{\gamma_m(2m)_\ell t^{2m-\ell}}
{1-q^m}z_N^mS_{r,m}(q).
\label{p8:eq:derivative-error}
\end{equation}
Thus the same $q$-Richardson table simultaneously cancels the first $r$ geometric tail powers in every logarithmic derivative.
\end{theorem}

For reconstructing derivatives of the product itself, set $L(t)=\log|P_b(t)|$. Fa\`a di Bruno's formula becomes
\begin{equation}
\frac{P_b^{(n)}(t)}{P_b(t)}
=\mathcal B_n\bigl(L'(t),L''(t),\ldots,L^{(n)}(t)\bigr)
\label{p8:eq:bell-log-derivatives}
\end{equation}
where the sign of $P_b$ is locally constant. Combining \cref{p8:eq:derivative-error,p8:eq:bell-log-derivatives} gives an accelerated hierarchy for all derivatives of the Fourier transform away from its zeros.

\subsection{Application to interzero extrema}

Between consecutive zeros of $\FourierTwoPiTransformOf{\RvachevUp}$, an extremum of its modulus satisfies
\begin{equation}
\frac{\ddPartll}{\ddPartll\xi}\log|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)|=0.
\label{p8:eq:peak-equation}
\end{equation}
\Cref{p8:eq:up-factor} separates the first sinc factor from $P_2(\pi\xi)$. Theorem~\ref{p8:thm:derivative-accel} gives a certified high-order approximation to the tail contribution in \cref{p8:eq:peak-equation}. This suggests a numerically stable alternative to multiplying many tiny sinc factors and then differentiating the product.

\section{Transfer to finite signed Thue--Morse sums}
\label{p8:sec:thue-morse-transfer}

Let
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon_n=(-1)^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)},
\label{p8:eq:epsilon}
\end{equation}
where $\BinaryDigitSum(n)$ is the binary digit sum. The standard finite product identity
\begin{equation}
\sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_n z^n
=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(1-z^{2^j})
\label{p8:eq:TM-poly}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Lean-verified finite complex block transforms]
\label{p8:thm:lean-complex-block-transforms}
For every $N\ge0$ and $u,a,\zeta\in\CPartll$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}u^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}\exp(n\zeta)
 &=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(1+u\exp(2^j\zeta)\right),
 \label{p8:eq:lean-weighted-complex-block}\\
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}u^{\BinaryDigitSum(n)}\exp(a+n\zeta)
 &=\exp(a)\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(1+u\exp(2^j\zeta)\right),
 \label{p8:eq:lean-weighted-complex-block-affine}\\
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_n\exp(n\zeta)
 &=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\exp(2^j\zeta)\right),
 \label{p8:eq:lean-signed-complex-block}\\
 \sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_n\exp(a+n\zeta)
 &=\exp(a)\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(1-\exp(2^j\zeta)\right).
 \label{p8:eq:lean-signed-complex-block-affine}
\end{align}
All four equations include the zero-bit block $N=0$: the sum has its single empty-word term
$n=0$ and the product is empty.  The unshifted identities read $1=1$, while the affine
identities read $\exp(a)=\exp(a)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The declarations, in the displayed order, are
\path{sum_range_two_pow_binaryWeight_cexp},
\path{sum_range_two_pow_binaryWeight_cexp_affine},
\path{sum_range_two_pow_thueMorseSign_cexp}, and
\path{sum_range_two_pow_thueMorseSign_cexp_affine} in
\path{ThueMorseComplexExponential.lean}.  Binary expansion turns the left side of the first
identity into independent include-or-omit choices for the powers $2^j$.  The affine forms
factor out $\exp(a)$, while the signed forms are the corresponding Thue--Morse
specialization.
\end{proof}

Thus the raw finite complex exponential product needed below is machine checked.  A
phase-scaled substitution turns it into the raw product in the next proof.  These four
declarations do not themselves perform the sine/sinc conversion or removable-origin
normalization, but that finite layer is separately formal in
\path{ThueMorseComplexProductBridge.lean}.  The accelerated infinite-product conclusions,
exponential-generating-function convergence statements, and parity words with
position-dependent digit weights are not consequences of either finite module.

\begin{proposition}[Finite Thue--Morse representation of $P_N$]
\label{p8:prop:TM-PN}
Define
\begin{equation}
T_N(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{2^N-1}\varepsilon_n
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\iiPartll tn}{2^{N-1}}\right).
\label{p8:eq:TN}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
T_N(t)=\iiPartll^N2^{-N(N-1)/2}
\ePartll^{-\iiPartll t(1-2^{-N})}t^N P_N(t),
\label{p8:eq:TN-PN}
\end{equation}
with the identity at $t=0$ understood by continuity. Hence, for $t\ne0$,
\begin{equation}
P_N(t)=2^{N(N-1)/2}\iiPartll^{-N}
\ePartll^{\iiPartll t(1-2^{-N})}t^{-N}T_N(t).
\label{p8:eq:PN-TN}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Insert $z=e^{-\iiPartll t/2^{N-1}}$ in \cref{p8:eq:TM-poly}. Reindex the factors to obtain
\[
T_N(t)=\prod_{k=0}^{N-1}(1-e^{-\iiPartll t/2^k}).
\]
Using
\[
1-e^{-\iiPartll a}=2\iiPartll e^{-\iiPartll a/2}\sin(a/2),
\]
sum the phases.  Finally, factor
\[
\prod_{j=1}^{N}\sin(t/2^j)
=t^N2^{-N(N+1)/2}P_N(t),
\]
which gives the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Accelerated infinite product from finite digital sums]
\label{p8:cor:TM-accelerated}
For $t\ne0$ and admissible $N$,
\begin{equation}
|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{r}(t)|
=\prod_{j=0}^{r}
\left(
2^{(N+j)(N+j-1)/2}|t|^{-(N+j)}|T_{N+j}(t)|
\right)^{\lambda_{r,j}(1/4)}.
\label{p8:eq:TM-accelerated}
\end{equation}
The exact error, bracketing, and bounds of \cref{p8:thm:exact-rich-error,p8:cor:brackets,p8:thm:error-bound} therefore become statements purely about a finite collection of signed Thue--Morse exponential sums.
\end{corollary}

This transfer is conceptually useful even when direct product evaluation is faster.  Its raw
complex-exponential core is now formalized by
\cref{p8:thm:lean-complex-block-transforms}, and the total finite sine/sinc normalization is
formalized by \path{ThueMorseComplexProductBridge.lean}; the analytic Richardson conclusions
remain a route for further formal treatment.

\section{Numerical verification and observed orders}
\label{p8:sec:numerics}

All values in this section were recomputed with 100 decimal digits. The reference value used 300 sinc factors; at the displayed precision its omitted tail is negligible relative to every table entry. We report absolute logarithmic error
\[
\varepsilon_{N,r}(t)=
\left|\log|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{r}(t)|-\log|P(t)|\right|,
\]
where $r=0$ denotes the raw truncation $P_N$.

\centering

\label{p8:tab:numeric-t3}

$N$ & raw $r=0$ & $r=1$ & $r=2$ & $r=3$ \\

3 & $7.8198398\,10^{-3}$ & $1.8359337\,10^{-6}$ & $2.4411880\,10^{-10}$ & $9.9424663\,10^{-15}$ \\
4 & $1.9535830\,10^{-3}$ & $1.1451700\,10^{-7}$ & $3.8045692\,10^{-12}$ & $3.8728157\,10^{-17}$ \\
5 & $4.8830986\,10^{-4}$ & $7.1537455\,10^{-9}$ & $5.9408270\,10^{-14}$ & $1.5117515\,10^{-19}$ \\
6 & $1.2207210\,10^{-4}$ & $4.4705340\,10^{-10}$ & $9.2810541\,10^{-16}$ & $5.9042379\,10^{-22}$ \\

The successive-$N$ error ratios approach
\[
4^{-1},\quad4^{-2},\quad4^{-3},\quad4^{-4}
\]
for levels $r=0,1,2,3$, exactly as \cref{p8:eq:leading-error} predicts. The signs also alternate: raw and level two are upper approximations in modulus; levels one and three are lower approximations.
