\midrule
1 & 4 & $2.1701954\,10^{-4}$ & $1.4129551\,10^{-9}$ & $5.2149215\,10^{-15}$ & $5.8978483\,10^{-21}$ \\
1 & 6 & $1.3563390\,10^{-5}$ & $5.5189741\,10^{-12}$ & $1.2730608\,10^{-18}$ & $8.9985206\,10^{-26}$ \\
5 & 4 & $5.4288846\,10^{-3}$ & $8.8466438\,10^{-7}$ & $8.1669316\,10^{-11}$ & $2.3096377\,10^{-15}$ \\
5 & 6 & $3.3909800\,10^{-4}$ & $3.4497407\,10^{-9}$ & $1.9894410\,10^{-14}$ & $3.5155982\,10^{-20}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For $t=3$ the product itself is
\begin{equation}
P(3)=0.585719176535068482912529262451\ldots.
\label{p8:eq:P3}
\end{equation}
Even at $N=2$, the third transformed level improves the raw log error by approximately ten decimal orders while using only truncations through $N+3=5$.

\section{Lambert phase-locked extraction of the endpoint oscillation}

\section{The all-orders endpoint template}
\label{p8:sec:lambert-template}

The repository's corrected endpoint analysis has the structural form
\begin{equation}
\log F(x)=\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)+\Psi(\lambda(x))
+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{A_m(\lambda(x))}{\lambda(x)^m}
+O\!\left(\lambda(x)^{-M-1}\right),
\label{p8:eq:endpoint-template}
\end{equation}
where $\Psi$ and the coefficient functions $A_m$ are $1$-periodic. The nonoscillatory main saddle exponent may be written in the normalization used in the repository as
\begin{equation}
\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x)
=-\frac{L}{2}\lambda^2+
\left(1+\frac L2\right)\lambda
-\frac12\log\lambda+C_F,
\qquad L=\log2,
\label{p8:eq:Wmse}
\end{equation}
with the convention-dependent constant $C_F$. The critical fact for the present construction is not the explicit constant but the periodicity of every $A_m$ in the same phase variable.

Define the reduced endpoint observable
\begin{equation}
Y(x)=\log F(x)-\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x).
\label{p8:eq:Y}
\end{equation}
Then \cref{p8:eq:endpoint-template} becomes
\begin{equation}
Y(x)=\Psi(\lambda)+\sum_{m\ge1}A_m(\lambda)\lambda^{-m}
\label{p8:eq:Y-expansion}
\end{equation}
in the asymptotic sense.

\section{Phase-locked Lambert nodes}
\label{p8:sec:lambert}

\subsection{Exact phase locking}

Fix $\lambda>1/L$ and define
\begin{equation}
\lambda_j=\lambda+j,
\qquad
x_j=x_j(\lambda)=(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)},
\qquad j=0,1,\ldots.
\label{p8:eq:phase-nodes}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Exact Lambert inversion on the lattice]
\label{p8:lem:phase-lock}
For every $j\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
\lambda(x_j)=\lambda+j.
\label{p8:eq:phase-inverse}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $1$-periodic function $G$,
\begin{equation}
G(\lambda(x_j))=G(\lambda).
\label{p8:eq:periodic-lock}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{p8:eq:lambda-inverse} gives the inverse relation exactly on the decreasing branch. Periodicity gives the second assertion.
\end{proof}

Thus the nodes are not merely close in phase; they have exactly identical phase. Their physical spacing is nongeometric:
\begin{equation}
\frac{x_{j+1}}{x_j}
=\frac{\lambda+j+1}{2(\lambda+j)},
\qquad
\log_2\frac{x_j}{x_0}
=\log_2\frac{\lambda+j}{\lambda}-j.
\label{p8:eq:x-spacing}
\end{equation}
This slight deviation from a dyadic ray is precisely what preserves the Lambert phase.

\subsection{Lagrange weights in reciprocal scale}

Set
\begin{equation}
h_j=\frac1{\lambda+j}.
\label{p8:eq:h-nodes}
\end{equation}
We interpolate $Y(x_j)$ as a formal/asymptotic power series in $h_j$ and evaluate at $h=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Phase-locked Richardson weights]
\label{p8:thm:lambert-weights}
The Lagrange weights for evaluation at $h=0$ from $h_0,\ldots,h_r$ are
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\omega_{r,j}(\lambda)
=\prod_{\substack{0\le k\le r\\k\ne j}}
\frac{-h_k}{h_j-h_k}
=\frac{(-1)^{r-j}}{r!}\binom{r}{j}(\lambda+j)^r.}
\label{p8:eq:omega}
\end{equation}
They satisfy
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}=1,
\qquad
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda+j)^{-m}=0
\quad(1\le m\le r).
\label{p8:eq:omega-cancel}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since
\[
h_j-h_k=\frac{k-j}{(\lambda+j)(\lambda+k)},
\]
each factor in the Lagrange product equals $(\lambda+j)/(j-k)$. Therefore
\[
\omega_{r,j}=\frac{(\lambda+j)^r}
{\prod_{k\ne j}(j-k)}
=\frac{(-1)^{r-j}}{j!(r-j)!}(\lambda+j)^r.
\]
The moment identities follow either from polynomial interpolation or from the fact that an $r$th finite difference annihilates every polynomial of degree below $r$.
\end{proof}

The first three levels are
\begin{align}
(\omega_{1,0},\omega_{1,1})
&=(-\lambda,\lambda+1),
\label{p8:eq:omega1}\\
(\omega_{2,0},\omega_{2,1},\omega_{2,2})
&=\left(\frac{\lambda^2}{2},-(\lambda+1)^2,
\frac{(\lambda+2)^2}{2}\right),
\label{p8:eq:omega2}\\
(\omega_{3,0},\omega_{3,1},\omega_{3,2},\omega_{3,3})
&=\left(-\frac{\lambda^3}{6},\frac{(\lambda+1)^3}{2},
-\frac{(\lambda+2)^3}{2},\frac{(\lambda+3)^3}{6}\right).
\label{p8:eq:omega3}
\end{align}

\subsection{All residual moments}

Let $h_n(a_0,\ldots,a_r)$ denote the complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial, characterized by
\begin{equation}
\prod_{j=0}^{r}(1-a_jz)^{-1}
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}h_n(a_0,\ldots,a_r)z^n.
\label{p8:eq:complete-homogeneous}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Formalization status.}
The finite formal-power-series content of
\cref{p8:eq:complete-homogeneous} is now Lean-backed.  For an arbitrary
finite alphabet, \path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean} defines the series
whose coefficient of degree $n$ is the corresponding complete homogeneous
evaluation, proves the adjoining-variable geometric recurrence over every
commutative semiring, and, over every commutative ring, proves both the
denominator-cleared finite-product identity and identification with
\texttt{PowerSeries.invOfUnit} of the denominator product.  This is a purely
formal coefficient identity: it does not evaluate the series at an analytic
argument, prove convergence, take a logarithm, or establish the
Bell/generalized-harmonic formula \eqref{p8:eq:h-Bell} below.

\begin{theorem}[Exact inverse-power moments]
\label{p8:thm:lambert-moments}
For $s\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda+j)^{-(r+s)}
=\frac{(-1)^r}{(\lambda)_{r+1}}
 h_{s-1}\!\left(\frac1\lambda,\frac1{\lambda+1},\ldots,
\frac1{\lambda+r}\right),
\label{p8:eq:lambert-moments}
\end{equation}
where $(\lambda)_{r+1}=\lambda(\lambda+1)\cdots(\lambda+r)$ is the rising factorial. In particular,
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda+j)^{-(r+1)}
=\frac{(-1)^r}{(\lambda)_{r+1}}.
\label{p8:eq:lambert-leading-moment}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substituting \cref{p8:eq:omega} reduces the left-hand side to
\[
\frac1{r!}\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{r-j}\binom{r}{j}(\lambda+j)^{-s}.
\]
For $s=1$, partial fractions of $1/(\lambda)_{r+1}$ give \cref{p8:eq:lambert-leading-moment}. Repeated differentiation with respect to $\lambda$, or multiplication of the generating function of reciprocal powers, yields \cref{p8:eq:lambert-moments}. Equivalently, compare coefficients in
\[
\frac{1}{(\lambda-z)_{r+1}}
=\frac{1}{(\lambda)_{r+1}}
\prod_{j=0}^{r}\left(1-\frac{z}{\lambda+j}\right)^{-1}.
\]
\end{proof}

Define generalized harmonic sums
\begin{equation}
H_k^{(r)}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^{-k}.
\label{p8:eq:Hrk}
\end{equation}
Then the complete homogeneous polynomial in \cref{p8:eq:lambert-moments} has the Bell representation
\begin{equation}
h_n\!\left(\frac1\lambda,\ldots,\frac1{\lambda+r}\right)
=\frac1{n!}\mathcal B_n\!\left(
0!H_1^{(r)},1!H_2^{(r)},\ldots,(n-1)!H_n^{(r)}
\right).
\label{p8:eq:h-Bell}
\end{equation}
This is the second exact Bell-polynomial bridge in the report: the first exponentiated a log error; the second resolves all residual moments of the Lambert extrapolator.

\paragraph{Formalization status.}
The finite identity \eqref{p8:eq:h-Bell} is now Lean-backed without using an
analytic logarithm or an infinite-series convergence argument.
\path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean} defines
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousPowerSum} and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousBellInput}; its division-free theorem
\nolinkurl{bellComplete_completeHomogeneousBellInput} is the generic finite
Bell identity, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}
is its normalized form over a commutative $\RationalNumbers$-algebra.
\path{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean} specializes this result through
\nolinkurl{shiftedReciprocalPowerSum},
\nolinkurl{shiftedReciprocalBellInput}, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_shiftedReciprocal_eq_bell}, and proves
both
\nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_card_add_eq_bell}
and the report-shaped $s\ge1$ theorem
\nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_bell}.
The shifted reciprocal definitions use total field inverses; the Bell
specialization imposes no positivity hypothesis, while the exact weighted
moment interfaces assume characteristic zero.
Finally, \path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtractionBell.lean} proves the exact
residual rewrites
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell}.  These are
finite algebraic identities; they assert no convergence for the infinite
residual series and add no new asymptotic estimate.

\subsection{Extraction of the periodic endpoint factor}

Define
\begin{equation}
\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}(\lambda)
=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\omega_{r,j}(\lambda)Y(x_j(\lambda)).
\label{p8:eq:Psi-hat}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Phase-locked extraction of $\Psi$]
\label{p8:thm:Psi-extraction}
Assume the all-orders expansion \cref{p8:eq:endpoint-template} holds uniformly along a fixed compact phase set. Then, as $\lambda\to\infty$ with its fractional part fixed,
\begin{align}
\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}(\lambda)-\Psi(\lambda)
&\sim \frac{(-1)^r}{(\lambda)_{r+1}}
\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}A_{r+1+s}(\lambda)
 h_s\!\left(\frac1\lambda,\ldots,\frac1{\lambda+r}\right)
\label{p8:eq:Psi-full-error}\\
&=\frac{(-1)^rA_{r+1}(\lambda)}{(\lambda)_{r+1}}
+O(\lambda^{-r-2}).
\label{p8:eq:Psi-leading-error}
\end{align}
In particular, the first $r$ inverse-$\lambda$ corrections are cancelled exactly.
Here the symbol $\sim$ in \eqref{p8:eq:Psi-full-error} has its finite-order
Poincar\'e meaning: for every fixed $S\ge0$, subtracting the terms
$s=0,\ldots,S$ leaves $O_r(\lambda^{-r-S-2})$.  It does not assert that the
displayed infinite series converges for any fixed $\lambda$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
At the phase-locked nodes, periodicity gives
\[
Y(x_j)=\Psi(\lambda)+\sum_{m\ge1}A_m(\lambda)(\lambda+j)^{-m}.
\]
The constant survives because the weights sum to one. Terms $1\le m\le r$ vanish by \cref{p8:eq:omega-cancel}. Write $m=r+1+s$ and apply \cref{p8:thm:lambert-moments}. A small uniformity point is essential: the absolute weight sum is $O_r(\lambda^r)$. To obtain the expansion through residual index $s=S$, apply the input all-orders theorem through order $2r+S+1$. Its transformed remainder is then $O_r(\lambda^{-r-S-2})$, while the finitely many undisplayed transformed coefficients start at the same order. This proves the displayed Poincar\'e expansion despite the growing weights.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Lean formalization status.}
The logarithmic theorem, in the finite-order sense just specified, is now
compiled.  The module \path{FabiusLambertPhaseLockedPullback.lean} proves
\path{tendsto_lambertPhaseLockedNode_smallArgument} and the arbitrary-order
single-node estimate
\path{log_fabius_phaseLockedNode_sub_WikipediaLambertExpansion_isBigO}.
The modules \path{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean} and
\path{LambertReciprocalAsymptotics.lean} provide, respectively,
\path{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_isBigO_pow} and
\path{sum_norm_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_isBigO_pow_atTop}, together
with the balanced product estimate
\path{shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_isBigO_atTop}.  These control
the complete-homogeneous residual and the polynomially growing row.

The report-facing definitions in
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtraction.lean} are
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedReducedSample},
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator},
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm}, and
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum}.  Under the dictionary
$\Psi=\texttt{negativeLaplacePsi}$ and
$A_m=\texttt{fabiusSaddleLogCoefficient}\ m$, the declaration
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_residual_isBigO} proves the
finite truncation of \eqref{p8:eq:Psi-full-error};
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_periodic_isBigO} is its
unsubtracted $O(\lambda^{-r-1})$ specialization;
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_firstOmitted_isBigO} is exactly
\eqref{p8:eq:Psi-leading-error}, and
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_tendsto_periodicAlong} gives the
fixed-phase limit along $\lambda=a+n$.  The moving-target Big-$O$ statements
hold on unrestricted real $\lambda\to+\infty$, so the fixed-phase result is a
corollary rather than an additional uniformity assumption.

There are two harmless indexing conventions.  Lean's residual parameter
$S_{\mathrm L}$ counts the first $S_{\mathrm L}$ terms, whereas the report's
$S$ is the last retained index; hence $S_{\mathrm L}=S+1$.  Lean also writes
the input partial sum as \texttt{range N}, so its remainder has order
$\lambda^{-N}$ and its highest retained coefficient is $N-1$.  The internal
choice $N=2r+S_{\mathrm L}+1$ is therefore
$N=2r+S+2$, with highest retained coefficient $2r+S+1$, exactly as used in
the proof above.  All these theorems keep $r$ and $S$ fixed; no growing-order
uniformity is claimed.  Their natural-number statements include the singleton
row $r=0$ and the empty residual truncation $S_{\mathrm L}=0$.

Exponentiating produces a multiplicative estimator of the periodic amplitude:
\begin{equation}
\PhaseLockedExponentialEstimator{r}(\lambda)
=\exp(\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}(\lambda))
=\prod_{j=0}^{r}
\left(\frac{F(x_j)}{\exp(\mathcal W_{\mathrm{MSE}}(x_j))}
\right)^{\omega_{r,j}(\lambda)}.
\label{p8:eq:Ahat}
\end{equation}
Its relative-error coefficients are again complete Bell polynomials applied to the logarithmic coefficients in \cref{p8:eq:Psi-full-error}.
The identity \eqref{p8:eq:Ahat} is an immediate mathematical exponentiation,
but this multiplicative estimator and its Bell relative-error hierarchy are
not part of the closed Lean phase-extraction tranche.

\subsection{Coefficient extraction beyond \texorpdfstring{$\Psi$}{Psi}}

The same nodes can estimate $A_1,A_2,\ldots$ by differentiating the interpolating polynomial at $h=0$. If $\ell_j(h)$ is the Lagrange basis for $h_j$, then
\begin{equation}
\PhaseLockedCoefficientEstimator{s}{r}(\lambda)
=\frac1{s!}\sum_{j=0}^{r}\ell_j^{(s)}(0)Y(x_j),
\qquad 0\le s\le r,
\label{p8:eq:As-extractor}
\end{equation}
with $\PhaseLockedCoefficientEstimator{0}{r}=\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}$. For $s=1$,
\begin{equation}
\ell_j'(0)
=-\omega_{r,j}\sum_{\substack{0\le k\le r\\k\ne j}}(\lambda+k).
\label{p8:eq:first-derivative-weight}
\end{equation}
Higher derivative weights are elementary symmetric polynomials in the numbers $-(\lambda+k)$ and can be generated by Bell polynomials. Thus a single phase-locked sample block can recover a triangular set of periodic coefficient functions.
These higher derivative extractors likewise remain a formalization target.

\section{Bernoulli-polynomial geometry and conditioning of the Lambert lattice}
\label{p8:sec:lambert-conditioning}

Unlike the geometric $q$-weights, the phase-locked weights grow polynomially in $\lambda$:
\begin{equation}
\kappa_r^{\mathrm L}(\lambda)
:=\sum_{j=0}^{r}|\omega_{r,j}(\lambda)|
=\frac1{r!}\sum_{j=0}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(\lambda+j)^r.
\label{p8:eq:lambert-condition}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, if $J\sim\mathrm{Binomial}(r,1/2)$,
\begin{equation}
\kappa_r^{\mathrm L}(\lambda)
=\frac{2^r}{r!}\Expectation(\lambda+J)^r
\sim_{\lambda\to\infty,\,r\,\mathrm{fixed}}\frac{2^r}{r!}\lambda^r.
\label{p8:eq:lambert-condition-asymp}
\end{equation}
Thus high precision is essential. This is not a defect of the derivation: extrapolating from $h_j\asymp1/\lambda$ to the remote point $h=0$ is intrinsically ill-conditioned.

The binomial moments can be expanded through Stirling numbers:
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}\binom{r}{j}j^k
=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}
\left\{\begin{matrix}k\\\ell\end{matrix}\right\}
r^{\underline{\ell}}\,2^{r-\ell}.
\label{p8:eq:binomial-moments}
\end{equation}
Here $r^{\underline{\ell}}=r(r-1)\cdots(r-\ell+1)$ is a falling factorial. For unweighted arithmetic-node diagnostics, Bernoulli polynomials give the exact power sums
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^p
=\frac{B_{p+1}(\lambda+r+1)-B_{p+1}(\lambda)}{p+1}.
\label{p8:eq:bernoulli-power-sum}
\end{equation}
\Cref{p8:eq:bernoulli-power-sum} is useful for scaling, centering, and constructing orthogonalized polynomial bases on the Lambert node block. It also makes explicit why Bernoulli polynomials are the natural arithmetic-lattice companion to the Bernoulli numbers already present in the sinc-tail coefficients.

\subsection{A stable evaluation strategy}

For practical extraction of $\Psi$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item compute $\lambda$ and $x_j$ at precision exceeding the target by roughly
$\log_{10}\kappa_r^{\mathrm L}(\lambda)$ guard digits;
\item evaluate $Y(x_j)$ with the same phase convention and main term;
\item use barycentric interpolation in $h_j$ rather than explicitly summing large alternating monomials;
\item repeat at successive $\lambda$ with the same fractional part and verify the predicted $\lambda^{-(r+1)}$ decay;
\item compare $r$ and $r+1$ transforms as an a posteriori diagnostic, while remembering that no sign bracket is presently available because the signs of $A_m(\lambda)$ are not known globally.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Unified interpretation, formalization path, and research program}

\section{Two extrapolation geometries}
\label{p8:sec:dual-geometries}

The two constructions are instances of one principle: choose a coordinate in which the defect has a power expansion, then interpolate to the zero-defect point.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Comparison of the two extrapolation geometries.}
\label{p8:tab:geometries}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l X X@{}}
\toprule
Feature & Sinc-tail transform & Lambert endpoint transform \\
\midrule
Defect coordinate & $z_N=q^{N+1}$ & $h=1/\lambda$ \\
Node family & $z_Nq^j$ & $(\lambda+j)^{-1}$ \\
Invariant structure & lacunary tail coefficients & periodic phase coefficients \\
Interpolation algebra & Gaussian $q$-binomial & ordinary binomial/finite difference \\
Leading cancellation & powers $z,\ldots,z^r$ & powers $h,\ldots,h^r$ \\
Coefficient source & Bernoulli numbers / $\zeta(2m)$ & periodic saddle jets $A_m$ \\
Reconstruction & exponential Bell polynomials & complete homogeneous and Bell polynomials \\
Conditioning & uniformly bounded in $r$ & grows like $2^r\lambda^r/r!$ \\
Certification & exact alternating brackets & asymptotic order, no global sign yet \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The sinc scheme is unusually favorable because the geometric-node Lagrange weights have bounded total variation. The Lambert scheme pays for exact phase locking with growing weights, but it solves a different problem: extracting a nonconstant periodic amplitude that ordinary dyadic extrapolation would mix across phases.

\section{Consequences for Fabius and Rvachev computations}
\label{p8:sec:consequences}

\subsection{Certified Fourier evaluation}

Using \cref{p8:eq:up-factor}, compute the first factor exactly and bracket $P_2(\pi\xi)$ by consecutive transformed levels. This produces a certified enclosure for $|\FourierTwoPi{\RvachevUp}(\xi)|$ away from zeros. Because the work is done in logarithms, the method remains usable deep in the log-normal decay regime where direct products underflow.

\subsection{Peak localization and envelope testing}

Use the derivative transform in \cref{p8:eq:derivative-error} inside \cref{p8:eq:peak-equation}. The resulting roots can test fine envelope conjectures without contaminating the comparison by truncation error. In particular, a claimed subleading Fourier envelope should be compared against a product value whose numerical error is below the proposed correction term; the bracket theorem makes that requirement checkable.

\subsection{Independent extraction of the endpoint oscillation}

The phase-locked transform \cref{p8:eq:Psi-hat} gives a second way to recover $\Psi$: not from its Fourier/Gamma--zeta representation, but from $F$ itself. Agreement between the two routes would be a stringent validation of constants, branches, and phase conventions in the endpoint asymptotic.

\subsection{A bridge from exact dyadic values to asymptotics}

The repository already relates exact dyadic values to a phase-locked Lambert coordinate on fixed-numerator rays. The new construction suggests a complementary experiment:
\begin{enumerate}
\item use exact or high-precision values of $F$ at nearby phase-locked non-dyadic nodes $x_j$;
\item extract $\Psi$ and several $A_m$;
\item compare them with the periodic coefficient functions inferred from exact dyadic sequences whose phases become dense;
\item use the discrepancy to isolate the dyadic-arithmetic contribution from the universal saddle contribution.
\end{enumerate}
This does not by itself prove the exact-to-asymptotic bridge, but it supplies a sharply conditioned diagnostic coordinate for that problem.

\subsection{Base-\texorpdfstring{$b$}{b} generalized up-functions}

Theorems~\ref{p8:thm:exact-tail}--\ref{p8:thm:error-bound} hold for every real $b>1$. If a compactly supported refinement function has Fourier transform
\(
\prod_{k\ge1}\SincRad(t/b^k)
\)
or a finite product times this tail, the same acceleration and bracketing apply with $q=b^{-2}$. This broadens the method beyond the dyadic Rvachev function and may be useful for nonbinary infinite convolutions.

\section{Lean formalization blueprint}
\label{p8:sec:lean}

The sinc-side results are well suited to formalization because their analytic and algebraic components separate cleanly.

\subsection{Suggested definitions and compiled Lambert crosswalk}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \texttt{lacunarySincProd b N t}: finite product over $k=1,\ldots,N$.
\item \texttt{lacunarySincProdInf b t}: infinite product, with convergence proved from $\sum t^2b^{-2k}$.
\item \texttt{qRichardsonWeight q r j}: the closed form \cref{p8:eq:qweights}.
\item \texttt{qRichardsonLog q r f N}: weighted sum of $f(N+j)$.
\item \texttt{lambertPhaseLockedNode lambda j}: the compiled definition
      $(\lambda+j)2^{-(\lambda+j)}$.
\item \texttt{shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight lambda r j}: the compiled
      Lagrange definition underlying \cref{p8:eq:omega}.
\item \texttt{shiftedReciprocalPowerSum lambda r k}: the compiled generalized
      power sum $\sum_{j=0}^{r}(\lambda+j)^{-k}$.
\item \texttt{shiftedReciprocalBellInput lambda r}: the compiled
      factorially weighted Bell input attached to that reciprocal alphabet.
\item \texttt{fabiusPhaseLockedReducedSample} and
      \texttt{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator}: the compiled forms of
      $Y(x_j)$ and $\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}$.
\item \texttt{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm} and
      \texttt{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum}: the compiled individual
      and finite residual expressions in \eqref{p8:eq:Psi-full-error}.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Algebraic theorem chain}

The absolute-weight, Lambert-weight/cancellation, reciprocal-moment, finite
complete-homogeneous generating-product, and Bell/generalized-harmonic tasks
in the post-snapshot plan are now discharged.  The generic finite-alphabet
bridge is
\nolinkurl{bellComplete_completeHomogeneousBellInput}, with normalized
report form
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}.
Its shifted reciprocal specialization is
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_shiftedReciprocal_eq_bell}; the two exact
moment interfaces are
\nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_card_add_eq_bell}
and \nolinkurl{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_bell}.
The extraction layer then rewrites an individual term and every finite
partial sum through \nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell}.  This closes the
finite algebraic packaging only; it neither sums the infinite residual series
nor strengthens the analytic phase-extraction estimates.

\paragraph{Post-snapshot algebraic progress.}
The later module \path{GeometricLagrange.lean} now formalizes Lagrange
evaluation weights for arbitrary distinct nodes, polynomial and module-valued
exactness, the first omitted monomial moment, and geometric-node
specializations.  The compiled bridge
\path{GeometricQBinomialLagrange.lean} now supplies the arbitrary-ratio
algebra needed here.  Its \path{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator} is
zero for \(j>r\), and for \(j\le r\) is definitionally written as
\( (-1)^{r-j}q^{\binom{r-j+1}{2}}
\texttt{gaussianBinomial}\ q\ r\ (r-j)\).  The division-free reflection
theorem \path{gaussianBinomial_symm} from
\path{FiniteQBinomialCore.lean} proves the standard forward-index formula,
globally after zero extension, as
\path{geometricQBinomialWeightNumerator_eq_forward}.  The core also proves
functoriality \path{map_gaussianBinomial}, both \(q\)-Pascal recurrences,
finite \(q\)-Pochhammer shift and concatenation laws, both denominator-free
product--Gaussian factorial identities, and the finite \(q\)-binomial
theorem.  The theorem
\path{reversed_finite_qBinomial_theorem} identifies the corresponding
sum with \(\prod_{k<r}(z-q^{k+1})\) without division.  Under
\path{Set.InjOn} of \(k\mapsto q^k\) on \(\{0,\ldots,r\}\),
\path{geometricLagrangeWeight_eq_gaussianBinomial_div} normalizes this
numerator by \((q;q)_r\), while
\path{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial} gives,
for every \(m>0\),
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}(q)(q^j)^m
 =(-1)^r q^{r(r+1)/2}
   \texttt{gaussianBinomial}\ q\ (m-1)\ r.
\]
Thus the weight formula and every positive residual moment, including the
\(q=1/4\) specialization whenever the nodes are injective, are no longer
formalization obligations.  In particular, the report's displayed
\(\GaussianBinomial{r}{j}{q}\) is now the literal forward-index theorem, while the
reversed form remains available for the product proof.  At \(q=1/2\),
\texttt{FabiusDiscreteLimitToeplitz.lean} identifies the same weights with
the half-base Toeplitz row and retains its all-degree reverse moments and
exact total variation.  The later rational geometric-moment layer also proves
the finite condition-number identity \cref{p8:eq:qcondition}; its infinite limit
and decimal evaluation, together with the concrete sinc-product error,
quantitative remainder, and uniform convergence claims, remain frontier.

The operator-level coefficient algebra is now reusable as well.
\path{FinitePowerSeriesFilter.lean} defines
\(\sum_{j\in S}w_j f(a_jX)\) over an arbitrary commutative semiring and
proves that its degree-\(m\) coefficient is multiplied by
\(\sum_jw_ja_j^m\); it also proves constant-term, exactness, rescaling, and
coefficient-map laws.  \path{GeometricPowerSeriesFilter.lean} specializes
to \(a_j=q^j\), proves cancellation through the exactness order, and gives
the Gaussian residual multiplier in every positive degree.  Its
\path{geometricLagrangeSeriesFilter_eq_residual_mk} records the degree-zero
mass boundary and all positive degrees in one formal-series identity.
\path{AnalyticSeriesFilter.lean} now supplies the direct evaluated-series
counterpart: under unconditional summability, the filter has an exact head/tail
split and its geometric-Lagrange form has the complete shifted Gaussian tail.
Only nonzero-weight samples require unweighted summability, so zero weights are
handled sharply.  The later \path{RvachevQBinomialFilter.lean} connects the
named even-moment coefficients to the actual infinite Rvachev sinc product and
gives the corresponding entire filter, assumption-free at quarter base.  It
does not connect the present logarithmic tail coefficients to the finite
lacunary-product truncations.  Conditionally-only boundary series, convergence
of those truncations, signs, bounds, brackets, asymptotics, and uniform
remainder control remain governed by the analytic frontier stated below.

At post-snapshot checkpoint
\nolinkurl{1e761ed77583e9870ceeaeb2c74ac824094f0f3f}, the algebraic route to the
same residual formula was strengthened and factored through complete
homogeneous polynomials.  The five reusable declarations added to
\path{CompleteHomogeneous.lean} are
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_smul},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_option_zero},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_fin_succ},
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range}, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalAt_zero}.  Over every commutative semiring,
\path{GeometricCompleteHomogeneous.lean} proves
\[
 h_n(1,q,\ldots,q^r)=\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{r}{q},
 \qquad
 h_n(c,cq,\ldots,cq^r)=c^n\GaussianBinomial{n+r}{r}{q}
\]
as \nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_geometric} and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEval_scaled_geometric}; the range-indexed form
is
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_range_pow_eq_gaussianBinomial}.

The later module \path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean} packages these
evaluations into a formal power series.  Its coefficient and empty-alphabet
interfaces are
\nolinkurl{coeff_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_empty}.  The theorem
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert} is the
adjoining-variable geometric recurrence over a commutative semiring.  Over a
commutative ring,
\nolinkurl{one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_insert} clears
one linear denominator,
\nolinkurl{prod_one_sub_mul_completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn} clears the
whole finite product, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousGeneratingSeriesOn_eq_invOfUnit_prod}
identifies the coefficient series with the canonical formal inverse of that
product.  These declarations close the formal finite-product content of
\eqref{p8:eq:complete-homogeneous}, including empty alphabets, repeated
values, zero divisors, and nilpotents; they assert no analytic convergence or
Bell/generalized-harmonic identity.

The separate module \path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean} supplies that finite
Bell identity.  Its semiring-cleared theorem
\nolinkurl{bellComplete_completeHomogeneousBellInput} avoids division, while
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}
gives the factorially normalized report form over a commutative
$\RationalNumbers$-algebra.  Thus the preceding disclaimer remains specific to the
generating-series module: neither module evaluates a formal series at an
analytic argument or proves convergence.

Over every commutative ring, the hypotheses of
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_succ_add} and
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos} impose exactness
directly on the displayed nodes \(c q^j\), not first on \(q^j\).  Thus zero
and zero-divisor scales require no cancellation.  Their unscaled companions
are \nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_succ_add} and
\nolinkurl{sum_weight_mul_geometric_pow_of_pos}.  The field-valued
distinct-node consequences are
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_succ_add},
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_pow_of_pos},
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_scaled_geometric_pow_of_pos}, and
\nolinkurl{sum_geometricLagrangeWeight_mul_shifted_pow_of_pos}.  In particular,
the generic scaled positive moment is exactly
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{r}w_j(cq^j)^m
 =c^m(-1)^r q^{\binom{r+1}{2}}
   \texttt{gaussianBinomial}\ q\ (m-1)\ r
 \qquad(m>0),
\]
provided the same row reproduces evaluation at zero through degree \(r\) on
those scaled nodes.  These sixteen declarations use no division at the
commutative-semiring or commutative-ring layers; finite-node injectivity enters
only in the Lagrange field corollaries.

The later rational layer is implemented in
\begin{center}
\texttt{GeometricRichardson.lean}\\
\texttt{GeometricLagrangeWeights.lean}\\
\texttt{GeometricLagrangeQBinomial.lean}\\
\texttt{GeometricLagrangeQMoments.lean}.
\end{center}
Together these modules identify the finite geometric Richardson polynomial
with its Lagrange row, prove the \(q\)-Pochhammer and Gaussian-binomial weight
formulas, and compute every rational \(q\)-moment
under the exact nonzero-base and nonvanishing-denominator hypotheses.  For
\(0<q<1\), they establish every residual sign and the finite exact
\(\ell^1\) norm.  The declarations
\path{quarterGeometricLagrangeQMoment_eq_residual_qBinomial} and
\path{sum_abs_quarterGeometricLagrangeWeight_eq_qPochhammer_ratio} provide
the report-facing \(q=1/4\) specializations.

Independently,
\texttt{ExponentialPartition.lean} and
\texttt{ExponentialBell.lean} identify the universal weighted-partition
formula for an exponential coefficient with
\texttt{SaddleExpansion.expCoeff} over every commutative
\(\RationalNumbers\)-algebra.  The later \texttt{MomentCumulantAlgebra.lean}
factorially normalizes the same engine into named complete-Bell and inverse
moment--cumulant transforms, with the classical Bell recurrence and
normalized inverse laws.  In particular, the coefficient mechanism behind
\eqref{p8:eq:h-Bell} is now instantiated in
\path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean}: the division-free complete-Bell identity
uses the factorially weighted finite power sums, and
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}
gives the normalized formula over a commutative $\RationalNumbers$-algebra.  No
analytic logarithm is used.  The geometric principal
specialization and all finite geometric residual moments are therefore no
longer formalization obligations.

Finally, \texttt{LambertPhaseLockedRichardson.lean} proves the exact
lower-Lambert phase locking, factorial and binomial reciprocal-grid weights,
their cancellation moments, and the report-shaped all-order identity
\path{sum_shiftedReciprocalLagrangeWeight_mul_invPow_eq_completeHomogeneous}.
Together with \path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean}, this closes the
finite formal generating-product layer.  Four further modules close the
logarithmic finite-order phase-extraction layer:
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseLockedPullback.lean} pulls the uniform endpoint
remainder to each fixed node;
\path{CompleteHomogeneousAsymptotics.lean} bounds the homogeneous residuals;
\path{LambertReciprocalAsymptotics.lean} controls the growing reciprocal row;
and \path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtraction.lean} combines these estimates with the
exact moments.  Its declarations
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_residual_isBigO},
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_periodic_isBigO},
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_sub_firstOmitted_isBigO}, and
\path{fabiusPhaseLockedPeriodicEstimator_tendsto_periodicAlong} give the full
finite Poincar\'e truncations, the raw error order, the leading formula, and
the fixed-phase limit, respectively.

The downstream module \path{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean} identifies the
shifted reciprocal Bell input and proves both exact Bell forms of the
reciprocal-grid moments.  The module
\path{FabiusLambertPhaseExtractionBell.lean} then proves
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell}.  Consequently,
the finite Bell/generalized-harmonic presentation \eqref{p8:eq:h-Bell} and its
exact finite residual rewrites are closed.

Outside this closed tranche remain convergence of the infinite residual
series, the multiplicative estimator's Bell relative-error hierarchy, the
higher derivative coefficient extractors, Lambert residual sign/bracketing
results, and bounds uniform when $r$ or the residual depth grows with
$\lambda$.  The transformed sinc-tail identities, analytic Richardson
bracketing, and infinite condition-number limits are separate sinc-side
frontier obligations.

\subsection{Remaining sinc-side analytic theorem chain}

The analytic side can then proceed as follows:
\begin{enumerate}
\item establish the log-sinc power series on $(-\pi,\pi)$;
\item justify the double-series interchange for the omitted tail;
\item derive the exact transformed error by finite linearity;
\item prove positivity of every factor in \cref{p8:eq:E-explicit};
\item deduce bracketing and the explicit majorant;
\item add termwise differentiation on compact zero-free intervals.
\end{enumerate}

The endpoint chain is independent of this remaining sinc-side list and is now
complete at fixed finite order.  Its exact phase and endpoint coefficients
come from \path{FabiusFullAsymptoticExpansion.lean}; its interpolation algebra
comes from \path{LambertPhaseLockedRichardson.lean}; and the four-module
pullback/asymptotic/extraction tranche listed above combines the growing row
with the uniform saddle remainder at the strength asserted in
\cref{p8:thm:Psi-extraction}.  The formal result is the family of finite
Big-$O$ truncations specified after that theorem, not convergence of the
displayed infinite residual series.

\section{Open problems and next frontier steps}
\label{p8:sec:open}

\subsection{Optimal order under finite precision}

For exact input logs, \cref{p8:eq:error-bound} improves supergeometrically with $r$, while \cref{p8:eq:qcondition} remains bounded. In floating-point arithmetic, however, the truncations $P_{N+j}$ are themselves computed recursively and share correlated rounding errors. A sharp forward-error theorem should combine:
\[
\text{analytic error }\asymp q^{r(r+1)/2}a^{r+1}
\quad\text{with}\quad
\text{roundoff }\lesssim\kappa_r(q)u.
\]
Because $\kappa_r(q)$ converges, the optimal $r$ may be much larger than in ordinary Richardson extrapolation.

\subsection{Monotonicity within each bracket parity}

The sign theorem places every even level above the limit in modulus and every odd level
below it, but it does not order two levels of the same parity.  In fact, such an ordering is
false over the full parameter range $b>1$.  To see this directly, take
\[
 q=\frac45,\qquad b=q^{-1/2}=\frac{\sqrt5}{2},\qquad N=0,
 \qquad \rho_0=\pi\sqrt{\frac{99}{100}},
 \qquad t=b\rho_0=\pi\sqrt{\frac{99}{80}}.
\]
This is admissible because $\rho_0<\pi$, and $P_{b,0}=1$ is the nonzero empty product.
Substitution in the absolutely convergent series \eqref{p8:eq:E-explicit}, with
$a=(\rho_0/\pi)^2=99/100$, gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|rrrr}
r&0&1&2&3\\ \hline
E_{b;0,r}(t)
& 13.5220607386&-12.9320597704&16.5469385988&-14.7912158217.
\end{array}
\]
Thus $E_{b;0,2}>E_{b;0,0}>0$ and
$E_{b;0,3}<E_{b;0,1}<0$: both proposed same-parity monotonicities fail.
These numbers are reproducible by summing the terms through $m=5000$ in
\eqref{p8:eq:E-explicit}.  The same majorization used in \cref{p8:thm:error-bound} bounds the
discarded tail, for each $0\le r\le3$, by
\[
 \frac{q^{r(r+1)/2}\zeta(2)}
 {(q;q)_r(1-q)(5001)}\frac{a^{5001}}{1-a}<2\times10^{-22}.
\]

The correctly parameterized open question is therefore: for which pairs
$(q,a)\in(0,1)^2$, and for which levels $s$, do
\[
 E_{2s}(q,a)>E_{2s+2}(q,a)>0,
 \qquad
 E_{2s+1}(q,a)<E_{2s+3}(q,a)<0,
\]
where $E_r(q,a)$ denotes the right-hand side of \eqref{p8:eq:E-explicit} after replacing
$(\rho_N/\pi)^2$ by $a$?  No nonempty parameter regime for these additional inequalities
is asserted here.  Whenever they do hold, consecutive same-parity levels sharpen the
certified brackets of \cref{p8:cor:brackets}.

\subsection{Global continuation across zeros}

The logarithmic construction is naturally local to a zero-free component. A global formulation could factor all explicitly known zeros from the truncations and extrapolate the analytic nonvanishing remainder. Such a theorem would give uniform certified evaluation across large frequency intervals, including neighborhoods of high-multiplicity dyadic zeros.

\subsection{Direct formulas for the phase coefficients}

The transformed moments \cref{p8:eq:lambert-moments} show exactly how $A_{r+1},A_{r+2},\ldots$ enter the extraction error. This invites an inverse problem: compute $\PhaseLockedLogEstimator{r}$ and $\PhaseLockedCoefficientEstimator{s}{r}$ at many phases, then identify their Fourier coefficients in terms of the Gamma--zeta data already present in the endpoint theory.

\subsection{A sign theory for Lambert corrections}

A sign pattern for $A_m(\lambda)$ would turn the phase-locked transform into a certified bracket analogous to \cref{p8:cor:brackets}. Even a sign theorem for $A_1$ or $A_2$ on a substantial phase interval would be useful.

\subsection{Thue--Morse arithmetic of transformed products}

For $q=1/4$, every exponent in \cref{p8:eq:r1-dyadic,p8:eq:r2-dyadic,p8:eq:r3-dyadic} is rational. Raising to a common denominator converts the approximant to a rational monomial identity in finite Thue--Morse sums. It is natural to ask whether the numerators and denominators exhibit systematic $2$-adic cancellation analogous to the exact dyadic arithmetic of $F$.

\section{Conclusion}

The repository already contains the ingredients of a remarkable analytic-combinatorial machine: Thue--Morse cancellation, Gaussian interpolation, exact dyadic arithmetic, infinite sinc products, Bernoulli cumulants, Bell reconstruction, and Lambert-$W$ endpoint geometry. The new results here arise by changing \emph{what} is extrapolated and \emph{where} the nodes are placed.

For the Fourier product, extrapolating logarithms exposes an exact positive power series in the geometric tail coordinate. Gaussian-$q$-binomial weights annihilate its first powers, and the remaining $q$-moments all have one sign. This yields high-order multiplicative approximants, exact alternating brackets, explicit bounds, bounded conditioning, Bell-polynomial relative errors, derivative acceleration, and a finite Thue--Morse realization.

For the endpoint asymptotic, moving by integer steps in the exact lower-Lambert coordinate freezes every periodic coefficient. Lagrange interpolation in reciprocal $\lambda$ then isolates the periodic factor and its correction hierarchy. The residual moments are not opaque: they are complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials, hence Bell polynomials in generalized harmonic sums, while Bernoulli polynomials govern the arithmetic-node geometry.

The two mechanisms are complementary. One is globally algebraic in the truncation index and exceptionally stable; the other is phase-sensitive and designed to separate a periodic asymptotic invariant. Both are sufficiently explicit to support immediate symbolic checking, high-precision computation, and a staged Lean formalization.

\section{Additional algebraic details}
\label{p8:app:details}

\subsection{Derivation of the \texorpdfstring{$q$}{q}-moment formula}

Starting from \cref{p8:eq:qweights},
\begin{align*}
S_{r,m}(q)
&=\frac{1}{(q;q)_r}
\sum_{j=0}^{r}(-1)^{r-j}
q^{\binom{r-j+1}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{r}{j}{q} q^{jm}\\
&=\frac{q^{rm}}{(q;q)_r}
\sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^k q^{\binom{k+1}{2}-km}\GaussianBinomial{r}{k}{q}\\
&=\frac{q^{rm}}{(q;q)_r}
\sum_{k=0}^{r}q^{\binom{k}{2}}\GaussianBinomial{r}{k}{q}
(-q^{1-m})^k\\
&=\frac{q^{rm}(q^{1-m};q)_r}{(q;q)_r}.
\end{align*}
For $m\ge r+1$,
\begin{align*}
(q^{1-m};q)_r
&=\prod_{k=0}^{r-1}(1-q^{1-m+k})\\
&=(-1)^r q^{\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}(1-m+k)}
\prod_{k=0}^{r-1}(1-q^{m-1-k})\\
&=(-1)^r q^{r(1-m)+r(r-1)/2}(q^{m-r};q)_r.
\end{align*}
Multiplication by $q^{rm}$ gives the exponent $r(r+1)/2$ in \cref{p8:eq:qmoment-positive}.

\subsection{Why all transformed sinc-tail terms have one sign}

The sign theorem is not a generic property of Richardson extrapolation. It relies on two positivity facts:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the log-sinc tail coefficients $d_m(t)$ are positive because $\zeta(2m)>0$;
\item after cancellation, every geometric moment is
\[
S_{r,m}(q)=(-1)^r\times
\frac{q^{r(r+1)/2}(q^{m-r};q)_r}{(q;q)_r},
\]
whose remaining factors are positive for $m\ge r+1$.
\end{enumerate}
Their product therefore has the same sign for every residual degree. This is the mechanism behind the certified brackets.

\subsection{Bell form of complete homogeneous functions}

The denominator-cleared formal product and its canonical formal inverse are
now proved in \path{CompleteHomogeneousGenerating.lean}.  The logarithmic
coefficient comparison in this subsection, including its specialization to
generalized harmonic sums, is not part of that generating-series result.
The separate \path{CompleteHomogeneousBell.lean} proves the same finite
coefficient identity by division-free Bell algebra and supplies its
factorially normalized form; \path{LambertPhaseLockedBell.lean} specializes
it to generalized harmonic sums.  Neither statement evaluates an infinite
formal series or proves analytic convergence.

Taking logarithms of \cref{p8:eq:complete-homogeneous},
\[
\log\prod_{j=0}^{r}(1-a_jz)^{-1}
=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^k}{k}\sum_{j=0}^{r}a_j^k.
\]
Comparing this with \cref{p8:eq:bell-gen} gives
\[
h_n(a_0,\ldots,a_r)
=\frac1{n!}\mathcal B_n\left(
0!p_1,1!p_2,\ldots,(n-1)!p_n\right),
\]
where $p_k=\sum_j a_j^k$. Substituting $a_j=(\lambda+j)^{-1}$ proves \cref{p8:eq:h-Bell}.
The corresponding compiled declarations are
\nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_eq_factorialNormalize_completeBellPolynomial}
and \nolinkurl{completeHomogeneousEvalOn_shiftedReciprocal_eq_bell}; the two
downstream residual rewrites are
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualTerm_eq_bell} and
\nolinkurl{fabiusPhaseLockedResidualPartialSum_eq_sum_bell}.

\section{Reproducibility notes}
\label{p8:app:repro}

The numerical checks used arbitrary-precision arithmetic with the following direct definitions:
\begin{align*}
P_N(t)&=\prod_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\sin(t/2^k)}{t/2^k},\\
P(t)&\approx\prod_{k=1}^{300}\frac{\sin(t/2^k)}{t/2^k},\\
\log|\DyadicProductExtrapolant{N}{r}(t)|
&=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\lambda_{r,j}(1/4)\log|P_{N+j}(t)|.
\end{align*}
At 100-digit working precision, the 300-factor reference tail is far below the displayed errors. Symbolic checks verified the moment identities through $r=8$ and multiple values of $m$ before the general proofs were written.
